The largest number of Japanese spinning mills
are in the Osaka-Kobe-Wakayama areas, where approximately

32 percent of the national total are concentrated. Aichi
and Gifu prefeotures have about 13 and 12 percent

Okoyama and Hiroshima prefectures about 6 peroent. The
remaining 29 percent are widely scattered over the
four 1slands of Japan Proper.  The weaving mills are
even more extensively dispersed.
In the Osaka area, in addition to the oity of

such cities and towns as Kishiwada, Haruki, Hirakata,
Sakai, Aza Ichimura, Sangenya, Sumiyoshi, Ozaki, Tsumori
and Kaizuka.

swajsct: Combined Production and Resources Beard
I am attaching & letter which I received
sometime ago from Sir George Sansom of the
British Embassy. Sir George informs me that
the British representative on the Combined
Production and Resources Board is proposing
a Combined Production and Resourees Board
Technical Commission be sent to Japan to
investigate Japan's textile industry. He
expresses the hope that the Department of State
will give its support to the proposal.
I imagine the British proposal will coms
to your office as it would be the appropriate
place to formulate State Department opinion
on the subject, and I am therefore sending
Sir George's letter to you.

Office of
BRITISH EMBASSY
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
WASHINGTON, D. C.
[ ]
September 21st, 1945
Dear John,
The British representative on the Combined
8
Production and Resources Board has been instructed by
the Minister of Production to approach his c∞lleagues with
a proposal that a Combined Production and Resources Board.
Technical Commission should be sent to Japan at once to
9
investigate the present position of the Japanese textile
5
industry.
55/9-2145
We are requested by the Foreign Office to
support this proposal, on the ground that the immediate
shortage of textiles, particularly in Far Eastern:
liberated areas, requires a rapid elucidation of the
position in Japan as to present stocks of raw materials,
yarn and cloth and as to the production capacity of the
industry in the near future. I need scarcely add that
there is also a further consideration, making it desir-
able that the fullest possible information should be
available at an early date to the Allied Powers con-
C
cerned. Such questions of policy as the extent to which
S
textiles can contribute to reparations and the future
scale of Japanese textile production for home consumption
and for export cannot be fully explored without exact
m
knowledge of the present state of the industry.
We hope therefore that the Departent of
894.65
State will give its support to the proposal for an
investigation upon a combined basis.
Yours ver mices
5
-2145
John Carter Vincent, Esqr.,
Office of Far Eastern Affairs,
United States Department of State,
Washington, D. C.

Mr. Vincent, Director of the Office of Far Eastern
Affairs, has asked me to reply to your letter of Septem-
ber l with respect to the interest of your Foreign Of-
fice in the proposal that the Combined Production and
Resources Boaru sponsor a technical mission to be sent
to Japan to stuay the Japanese textile position.
This office has been discussing this question with
CS/LE
Mr. William Batt, representins the Combined Production
and Resources Boara. We are now engaged in making ar-
rangements to secure the information on the Japanese
textile inaustry necessary to determine the extent to
Which the Japanese can aid in the present world shortage
of textiles and the policies wnicn shoula be followed
With respect to the future of te Japanese textile in-
austry. Our arrangements will take full account of the
great interest which the important consumers and pro-
aucers of textiles, especially in the Far East, have in
894.655
these questions.

MUSCOGEE MANUFACTURING COMPANY
COLUMBUS, GEORGIA
October 8th, 1945
Honorable Stephen Pace
Congress of the United States
House of epresentatives
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mir. Pace:
I thought you would be interested in the enclosed article on the
Japanese textile situation. We understand that the rayon
manufacturers have made a strong plea with our governament to
stop the operation of synthetic fiber textile plants on the
basis that these can be readily converted to war industries.
He have been unable to find out how much the cotton textile
industry in Japan was destroyed by bonbing. If you can get this
information we would certainly like to have it.
Before the war, we had practically lost our export business of cotton
goods account of Japanese competition and they were making rapid
inroads in our domestic business. I saw one towel in New York
which was a copy of a towel of ours. They were offering it
at seventy-two (72) cents a dozen and our cost on our towel
was around one dollar (1.oo) per dozen.
If we are  oing to do any export business and operate our mills
and use our cotton products, we will not be able to compete with
Japanese mills that are sub idized by their government and
paid what would be starvation wages in this country.
It seems to me that if : good part of this industry has been
destroyed, something can be done to have them only supply cotton
textiles for their own consumption and probably some nearby
Asiatic countries from which they get necessary food supplies.
With kindest regards, we are,
Yours very truly,
MUSCOGEE MANUFACTURING COLPANY
/s/ E. W. Swift
PHESIDENT AND JALES MANAGER

INDUSTRY: RAW SILK CONTHOL ASSOCIATION TO CONTINUE.
"The silk industry occupies a most important position in
the reconstroction of Japan. Following is Mr. Yamazoe's talk:
655/10-154
" 'Everywhere there is talk of controlled economy being
dropped, but if we are to adjust the operation costsand the
cost of cocoons to fit raw silk prices, and to secure increased
produrtion, it is considered some form of control will be
hecessary. Therefore, it is planned to continue the Japan
Raw Silk Control Association. However, the no. I raw silk
company "Japan Raw Silk Manufacturing Company" will be dis-
solved, and it is hoped that things will return to the posi-
tion of 1943. Regarding repayment of ioans, those enabled to do
5
so should forfeit their privileges in favor of others.
"If the qoota of raw materials that was in effect in 1942
can-be secured, that would be satisfactory. At present the
quantity cf ra si1k in stock is 60,000 bales, of which 45.000
894.65
are for export. These can be delivered at any time, according
to the demands of the Allied Headquarters. All of the raw silk
now being produced is being set aside for export by order of
Allied Headquarters.
"The necessity is felt for setting a production mark, but
as much depe-ds on the food situation, it is tco early to speak
of any independent measures.'

October 26, 1945.
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
W. L. CLAYTON
Mr. Will L. Clayton,
OCT 2 9 1945
Assistant Seoretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
655
A-C
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Dear Mr. Clayton:
Ever since news reports started coming from Japan
0
as to the condition of the textile industry there, wach succeeding
news dispatch has only served to confuse the situation rather than
N
clarify it. Reports of Japanese spokesmen claim that approximately
10,000,000 spindles have been destroyed which would mean that
3,O00,O00 spindles are at present in place. Colonel Kramer of
MacArthur's staff, on the other hand, estimates that only 1,000,000
spindles have been left in operating condition.
No concrete information is obtainable as to how
either of these figures were arrived at, but we were informed in
C
the case of the American estimate that it was based on srial
S
photographs from which the percentage of destruction of industrial
7
areas was estimated rather than on plant inspection,which,
after all, is the only acourate method.
Dispatches from Tokyo appearing in reputable New York
papers during the past week state that "The Tokto Textile Control
Association, together with private cotton businessmen, is busily
preparing for the importation of approximately 1,250,000 bales of
cotton next year with a view to supplying 25 yards of cotton goods
per capita to the Japanese population.
E
FILED
Translated into finished products, this proposed
entire annual foreign trade in cotton goods in the thirties.5
Statements such as these certainly serve only to confuse everyone
because how can Japan produce almost as much as she did pre-war with
only 20 to 25% of her pre-mar spindles.
The average person can also be pardoned for asking
why the Japanese should be figuring on 25 yerds per person when
according to all we know of the thinking about Japan is that they
are supposed to be on an austerity program, and when in pre-war

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
years the Philippines, which has a hot climate compared te Ja
consumed only about ló yards of cotton goods per capita ami
The future of the Japanese textile industry and to what
extent it is to be permitted to rebuild is of vital importanee to
the cotton manufacturing industry of the United States as well as
to the economy of the United States. It is likewise a matter of
paramount importance to our friends and allies, Great Britain.
We feel that in fairness to the industries of both
countriss our manufacturers should be informed now as to what the
high-level, over-all, long-range, plans are for the future of the
Japanese textile industry. We feel it is a matter of absolute
necessity that this be decided now when plans on post-war reeon-
version and rehabilitation of war-depreciated machinery are being
considered.
Your Department is the only one in a position to give
us this information. May we not, therefore, beg of you to give
this request the consideration it deserves and allow us the benefit
of at least some of your thinking on this problem?
We understand that one or several government missions will
soon go to Japan for the purposes of appraising and controlling the
entire industrial structure there -- not only the cotton and rayon
industries. May we suggest that the findings of this mission, as
well as the working out of any production program, whether interim
or long-range, is of great interest to our industry. I am sure that
you can appreciate we would be more than happy to cooperate in the
procurement of the necessary textile men -- men who are fully
capable to do the job that will be expeoted of them -- if we were
requested to do so.
We would be more than glad to be given the privilege of
conferring with you, together with representatives of the mammfao-
turing end of the cotton textile industry, so that a program, or
at least information, be disseminated which will clarify the whole
Japanese textile picture and permit of the making of plans for
the future.
Your early reaction to these suggestions will indeed be
very greatly appreciated.
Very truly yours,
DCR .  Unit
Am. E.Hand
Anal.
Rev.
Cat.
President.

LAWRENCE ILFELD & CO.

200 Summer Street

November 23, 1945
Mr. Hawthorne Arey, Vice President
Export-Import Bank
894.655/11-23
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Arey:
I am addressing you at the suggestion of the Foreign Department
of the First National Bank of Boston.
I have in mind that sooner or later the Japanese wool spinning
mills will recuire raw wool to manufacture yarns for fabric for domestic
consumption and possibly for export. In prewar days, this wool came
principally from Australia and was bought by Japanese representatives
at auctions there.
35
It is my thought that this direct method of purchase will not be
either permissible or feasible today and that the purchases will have
to be made either through British or American agencies. It seems likely
that in consideration of the financial situation and of the American
sphere of influence in Japan, this buying might be done through American
agents, who, like ourselves, have buying connections in Australia. Our
particular Australian agent was a leading operator for a Japanese
account before 1939 and is well versed in their requirements.
If my prospective outline is correct and the business is to be
controlled by your bank, I should like to be advised how we may parti-
cipate in this business. I shall appreciate it if you will give me
authentic information or if the business is to be done by another ageney,
you will refer me to someone who can and will give me concrete facts
so that unnecessary correspondence and vague abstractions may be avoided.
Thanking you in advance for your personal consideration, Im
Very truly yours,
心
71945
/s/ Lawrence Ilfeld

Dear Mr. Thorp:

Mission was awaiting me on my return from Washington this morning. I
believe Mr. Martin must have told you that ir. Rose, Mr. Comer, and I
were with the Army and with Mr. Martin and Mr. Olson on Monday, and I
think things are cleared as far as we are concerned on this trip.
We all appreciate the seriousness of this Mission and I can assure
you we will do our utmost to get together such facts and figures as are
possible and pertinent.
Personally, I em delighted that Mr. Fred Taylor with the Department
12-1245
of Agriculture is to be on the Mission, as he is an old-time personal
friend of mine and, of course, you know his capabilities both as.a
textile engineer and as a compiler of reports. Very frankly, I think
Mr. Taylor's being a partyon this Mission will in many ways be the
making of the Mission, for he has had the benefit of nearly six years
residence in China and Japan and, of course, knows a great deal about
the Japenese cotton industry.

THE JAPANESE TEXTILE DNDUSTRY
A. -PRODUCTION PROGRAM
I. Position of the Japanese Textile Industry in the
Pre-war and War Periods
Before the war Japan had a large textile industry which not only
supplied the domestic needs of the Japanese people but also provided
Japan with ber major source of foreign exchange. During the thirties
Japam exported more than half of her textile production. In fact, tex-
tiles constituted Japan's chief industry employing some million and one-
half people and exporting goods valued at more than 1,600,000,000 yem in
1939. This sum equalled 41 percent of the value of all Japan's exports
for that year.
Japan was dependent on large imports of textile raw materials, how-
ever, silk being the only textile raw material which Japan produced in
relative abundance. All the cotton, all the wool, most of the high grade
sulphite pulp for rayon, and many industrial fibers such as jute were im-
ported. These textile raw material imports cost Japan over 600,000,000
yen in 1939 or 20 percent of the value of all imports for that year. Futher-
more, most of these textile rew material imports came from non-Asiatic
sources. Japan depended on the United Stated and the British Empire for 70
percent of her raw cotton and on the British alone for 80 peroent of her
rew wool.
Japan has tried to free herself from dependence om imported raw meterials
by fostering the production of needed textile raw materials within her sphere
of domination. She has tried to increase cotton and wool production in occu-
pled China, Foree and Mancharia but production in these areas has actually
declimed rather then increesed due to war conditions, lack of fertiliser,
sabotage, and inadequate incentive goods to elicit the supply.
CONFIDENTIAL
14-000 P7 

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.97)
In the field of industrial fibers Japan has become largely
self-sufficient. Though the aree under Japanese control mas able
to produce a sufficient volume of industriel fibers to meet its
pre-war needs it has been necessary to substitute certain fibers,
such as flax (Soft Hemp) found in abundant supply within the aree,
for other fibers, notably jute, which normally have to be imported
into this region. Japan also curtailed the use of industrial fibers
to such a point that the estimated consumption of industrial fibers
in Japan in 1943 was approximately equal to the estimated production
of industrial fibers within the Japanese islands. To achieve this
self-sufficiency Japan increased the production of flax and other
industrial fibers in Japan proper. The Japanese claimed to have
developed means of using such fibers as hemp and abace in the
production of clothing textiles but practically all of the industrial
fibers produced in Japan in the last three years were used by the
a1
Japen's chief and most effective program for increasing the
supply of textile raw materiale within her empire has been in the
production of sulphite pulp for rayon and staple fiber yarn using
the viscose process. In 1937 Japan produced only 57,300 tons of
sulphite pulp within her empire. Production increased steadily until
it reached an estimated 325,000 tons in 1943. To do this Japan had
to increase her pulp mill capacity which reached 185,700 tons in
1 The Japanese radio boasted of having achieved considerable success
in producing textiles out of seaweed, mulberry oark, and weeds of tarious
kinds. However, there is no evidence that these measures have had
any appresiable effect on the total textile situation.
2/ Japan possessed far greater pulp mill capacity for producing paper pulp
than for producing synthetic fiber pulpl the two milling processes being
distinct from each other and using different kinds of wood can not be
easily interchanged.
16-008 P000

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.98)
Most of this increase has been in Hokkaido and Karafuto, though some has
been on the main island of Honshu and Kjushu and a small amount in Manchuria
and Korea. Sinoe sulphite pulp is also used to make plastics and nitrocellu-
lose, not all of Japan's high grade pulp has gone into staple fiber and rayon
yarns. To keep these pulp mills in production Japan had to cut deeply into
her limited supply of timber capable of producing this type of pulp. Since
staple fiber has greater durability and strengtn than rayon, the artificial
fiber industry has concentrated for the most part on staple fiber as opposed
to rayon.
Since Pearl Harbor the Japanese textile industry has been transformed from
a large industry dependent for nine-tenths of its raw materials on imports from
non-Asiatic sources to a relatively small industry using raw materials produced
in Japan or in the adjacent occupied areas. The greater part of the textile
raw materials available to Japan in 1943 were produced in the main Japanese
islands plus Hokkaido and Karafuto.
The war brought a progressive deterioration in the quality of textiles
produced as rayon and staple fiber supplanted cotton and wool. Stocks of
cotton and wool either stockpiled in Japan at the time of Pearl Harbor or
seised throughout the Orient in her subsequent conquests, have been gradually
depleted. More and more rayon and staple fiber was mixed with the dwindling
supplies of natural fibers until all civilian cloth became l00 percent artifi-
cial fiber and silk. Even the army had to resort to mixed fabrics containing
a considerable proportion of rayon or staple fiber. The small amount of wool and
cotton which Japan was able to extract from the ocoupied areas was of poor quality.
Even her synthetic fibers are inferior in strength and durability to the pre-
war product which was made largely of imported pulp of a higher cellulose
contont.
Japan has attempted to produce rayon pulp out of
24-00 P0 

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.98)
but these sources of rayon pulp have proved to be mmeh inferior to
wood. Some silk was mixed with rayon and staple fiber to give those
fibers greater strength. However, silk is too expensive a tertile material
and the supply of silk too small to compensate ia any appreciable wmy fer
the loss of cotton and wool.
As the supply even of this poor quality cloth was considerably
below the pre-wer level of tertile consumption in Japen, it became
necessary to institute textile rationing in 1942. Since then the
supply of tertiles available to the civilian population has shrunk and
the ration had to be cut. / Japan had to supply a limited amount of
textiles to the occupied areas to clothe the puppet troops and provide
an incentive to the production of essential raw materials by the subjeot
peoples. Wost of Japn's war-time tertile production went into military
and industrial uses, the army taking practically all the limited amounts
of cotton and wool. The military and industrial demand had first call
on the Japanese tertile industry, then the Japanese civilian population,
after them the so called "essential" people in the ocoupied areas (puppet
troops and subject people producing essential raw materials for Japan,)
and last of all the millions of "non-essential" peoples under Japamese
domination.
IL. Processing facilities cf the Japanese Textile Industry.
The Japanese textile industry was amply supplied with the most
modern tertile machinery and equipment. To be sure, a good deal of
17 For an account of the Japanese silk industry during the wer see page
 See Civil Affairs Guide; Price Control and Rationing of Consumer
Goods other than Food.
14-008 P20 b.

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.97)
the weeving was done on a very all soale with less up-to-date mmehimery
but the Japanese spinning industry has been famous for its efficient
performance. Japan has long had processing facilities far in exmess
of her own consumption needs. Even before the war, when Japan exported
over half of her total tertile production,her tertile industry was not
run at capacity.
Since the war, with export markets closed 'and home consumption
restricted, less and less of Japen's tertile equipment had been utilised.
It 1s estimated that less than 50 percent of Japans synthetio fiber
capacity and between 20 to 30 percent of Japan's ootton spinning
capacity were in use in l943. Since that year even a smaller proportion
of Japan's tertile capacity has been used. Silk production also suffered
a substantial decline since Pearl Harbor and even prior to Pearl Harbor
there wms considerable excess capacity in the highly competitive silk
reeling industry. It is estimated that by August 1944 the entire Japanese
textile industry in operation producing tertiles amounted to only 30
percent of Japan's pre-war tertile capacity.
Thès sharp reduotion in tertile production was due not only to
the scaroity of raw materials and the lack of erport markets since
Pearl Harbor, but also to the need to use all available facilities
and manpower in war production. Textile mills were converted to war
industries making airoraftjparts, machines and machine tools of all
kinds, rubber and chemical factories, etc. Cotton spinning plants
and synthetic fiber plants were particularly valuable in this respect,
since they are large modern plants with access to adequate power and
transportation facilities and with a trained labor fbrce alreadv .t
hand.

Idle spinning and weeving equipment was converted into mmchine tools

machine. By the summer of l944(as a result of the tertile reorganismatiom
in the spring of that year which the government promised would be the last

It is estimated that l0 percent of Japan's pre-war tertile capecity
was shipped overseas to outposts of the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity
Sphere. Lore was earmarked for future shipment but it is doubtful
whether any more than this 10 percent was actually sent. One reason
for this overseas textile program was to have processing facilities
near sources of raw materials and ultimate consumers thus reducing
shipping-space requirements. A more basic reason, however, was the intent
of Japan's military and industrial rulers to make of the Japanese
islands the heavy industry center of the empire using raw materials
produced throughout the empire and thus making Japan the essentiel
economic pivot of the entire area under Japanese domination. A
by-produot of this policy was a drastic reduction in Japan's textile
industry as compared with Japan'spre-war textile industry and the
building up of tertiles and other light industries at appropriate points
in the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere.
The leading

throughout the occupied areas to utilize this tertile equipment.

1. Japanese Supplies of Natural Fibers.
a. Cotton and Wool. - Since Japan produces practically mo

for the importation of these natural fibers. These fibers would have

8
Korea, fell short of their own requirements.
.b. Silk.- To meet the war emergenoy the silk industry has
been transformed from an industry supplying 80 percent of the worid's
silk exports to an industry producing textiles for home consumption.
Instead of producing fine counts for luxury materials, the industry
has shifted to producing course counts for greater strength and
durability. As a result silk consumption Japen has doubled. Luch of
it has gone into military uses. For a while the government encouraged
the use of silk in clothing tertiles by enabling civilians to buy silk
goods with fewer ration points than were required to buy synthetic
fabrics. As Japan's textile situation has deteriorated, it became
necessary to put the same point value on silk as on other fabrics.
The production of silk declined during the war despite the
urgent neeå for silk as a substitute for cotton and wool and as a
means of strengthening Japan's synthetic fibers. By 1945 silk-pro-
duction had decreased 40 percent from the average for the thirties, from
Japan has attempted since 1941 to produce both ootton and wool in
Japan proper. There is no evidence that the production of these
raw materials has represented more than a token amount.
14-000 P18 0

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
57,000 tons (including waste silk), which ms the averege for the
years 1934 to 1938, to an estimated 34,000 tons in the fiscal year
1944-'45. The chief cause for this decline has been the need to
convert acreage in mulberry trees to food crops in order to attain
the highest possible degree of self-sufficiency in food produotion.
Soon after Pearl Harbor hundreds of thousands of acres in mulberry
trees were uprooted. There is some evidence that the Japanese
authorities considered that the program for cutting down silk pro-
duction had gone too far as in the spring of l944 they tried to
stimulate cocoon production by more favorable prices and other aids
to cocoon producers. The crop continued to decline, however, duet
to diminishing supplies of fertilizer and the acute labor shortage
on the farms. It was thought necessary to uproot additional mulberry
trees in the spring of 1945, thus reducing silk production still
further.
Many of the factors causing the decline in cocoon production
during the war will be operative in the immediate period after the
war though there will no longer be a labor shortage. There will be
the same emphasis on producing the maximum amount of food possible.
Furthermore, once areas containing mulberry trees have been cleared,
it takes several years to bring them back into production again.
Therefore it is unlikely that Japan will be able to increase her silk
production during the immediate post-surrender period.
If silk production can be maintained at somewhere near the
1944-'45 level of 34,000 tons (187,500 tons of co00ons), Japan will
possess a substantial addition to her limited supplies of textile
raw materials. Before Pearl Harbor silk was an important source of

foreign exchange for Japan. Approximately three-fourths of all Japan's
silk went into the export market, most of it going to the United States
where it was consumed by the silk stocking industry. While the future
of real silk is obscure in the light of the recent development of
nylon, it is probable that silk will again become an important source of
foreign exchange for Japan thus enabling her to import raw cotton.
c. Industrial Fibers.- If Japan is able to maintain her 1945 rate of

will have an ample supply of flax, ramie and some hemp which will

depending upon the loss of Karafuto's industrial fiber production.)
Though this amount falls far short of Japan's pre-mar consumption

estimated amount of industrial fibers consumed in 1943. It will probebly
provide Japan with an adequate amount of industrial fibers during the
immediate period of occupation because of the decline in industrial
activity from its war-time peak and cessation of all the military dememds
on the industry. Barring imports,it will be necessary for Japan to
continue to substitute what industrial fibers she is able to grow at
home for other industrial fibers such as jute which can not be
produced in Japan in appreciable quantities.
d. Waste Fibers.-The amount of waste fibers Japan has at her disposal
is not known.

Japan used to obtain most of her imported salt from regions
controlled by European powers. With the drive toward self-sufficiency
within the yen bloc during the thirties, the Japanese increased the
production of salt in neighboring regions under Japanese control, the
Kwantung leased territory in Manchuria. By l937 most of
Japan's salt imports came from these regions. According to a Japanese
claim,the l944 salt supply available to Japan was more than double the
1937 supply. However, with mounting shipping and transportation
difficulties impeding the flow of goods from the mainland to Japan,
salt became a serious bottleneck and the Japanese government tried to
increase the production of salt within Japan proper. All restrictions
on the production of salt for home consumption were removed(salt had been
a goternment monopoly). Despite this effort salt produotion deoreased

B. CONTROL AND ADLINISTTATION OF TE JAPANESE TEXTILE INDUSTRY
I. Pre-War Period
1. Pre-war Structure of the Industry
The Japanese textile industry, the mainstay of Japan's economic
life in the pre-war years, embodisd many of the salient contradictions and
inconsistencies of Japan's recent development. On the one hand there were a
few very large firms, (engaged in spinning and weaving) capitalized at
millions of yen and employing thousands of workers. These firms owned
numerous plants equipped with the best modern textile machinery and had
close connections with Japan's dominant financial and industrial oligarchy.
At the other extreme were hundreds of thousands of small shops (weaving,
dyeing, and finishing) which employed less than 10 workers who generelly
lived in the craftsman's own home, and used primitive hand machinery or
very simple power-driven machinery adapted to such conditions. Between
these two extremes were the medium sized firms which employed anywhere
from 10 to several hundred workers (depending on the branch of the in-
dustry) and which used modern equipment, though as a rule not quite such
efficient machinery as that used by the large firms. ost of the medium
sized firms owmed only one plant and were confined to one type of operation:
weaving or spinning of cotton, rayon, woolen, or silk.
Despite the great number of small and medium sized units, it was
the large tertile companies which domineted the industry. The six largest
firms in cotton spinning or the "Big Six"1/ averaged 666,000 spindles each

In a sense the hundreds of thousands of small units in the Japanese
weaving and textile finishing industry reinforced the control of the industry
by the dominant textile interests. Not only were small units used as sub-
contractors, thus complementing rather then competing with larger firms,
but in so far as small establishment did compete with larger firms they
tended to compete with the medium sised firm rather then with the textile
giants. Small scale handicraft production with its outmoded methods of
production was able to survive into twentieth century Japan largely because
of the lack of adequate employment opportunities in modern Japanese industry.
Consequently home work continued to flourish with the self-employed crafts-
man or piece worker lowering his own wage and that of his family and appren-
tices to the vanishing point in order to compete with factories equipped
with modern machinery. Despite the low wages in these small textile shops,
the propriators of these establishments have tended with each business re-
cession to fall hopelessly into debt to their suppliers of raw mmterials or
their selling outlets, - wholesalers and exporters. Thus in effect these
small industrialists existed on the sufference of the dominant textile
interests which chose to maintain them in nominal independence.
14-088 788 bu

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
2. Organisation of the Textile Industry.
The medium sise plant, on the other hand, did compete with the large
textile firms and, in the ase of weaving, with the numerous small units as
well. The overhead costs of the medium sised plant could not be soaled dom in
time of depression or their wage bill cut to the same degree as wes possikle
in the small shop of less than ten employees which were not subject to the
factory acts covering hours and working conditions of women and minors.
In additiom to the obvious disadvantage of less efficient mohinery,
medium sised firms suffered other marked disadvantages in competition with
the large textile compenies. By tieir manifold operations in spinning and wcav-
ing, ir cotton, rayon, wool and spun silk, to say nothing of their varied
business activities and affiliation, big firms were able te withstand price
fluctuations and industrial vicissitudes far better than the independest firm
which specialized in one product and operation. Futhermore, the giant firms
with many plante were able not only to reap the advantages of large soale oper-
ations in buying, selling and obtaining credit but also were in a position to
exert subtle pressure on price relationahip through their econoomic and finan-
cial tieups with the big banks, exrorting and importing firms, ete.: The medium
sised firm was cnly able to maintein iteelf in competition with the giants by
catering to specialty export markets (as opposed to the standard white and gray
goods) and to the Japanese demand for goods of distinctive pattern and design.
2. Organization of the Textile Industry
The hierarahy of organizations in the Japanese te:tile induutry befor the
war refleoted the basic structure of the industry. The oldest and most powerful
organization was the Japenese Cotton Spinners' Associaticn (Dai Nippon Boseli
Rengo-Kai) organized in 1882 and dominated ever since by the giant spinning
weaving firms. This organization enabled leaders of the Japanese cottom
14-00 P00

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
spinning industry to extend their influeone over spinning companies mot
directly under their control and to bring the medium sised firms, the
so-called independants,into line on price output and production polieies.
Output limitations were resorted to on an extensive scale during the
depression of the thirties.
While such restrictions are unpopular and tend to become a weapon
directed by the leading firms in the industry against the maller firms,
there was little incentive for the sirty lesser firms in cotton spinning to
withdraw from the Japan Cotton Spinners' Association which included
no associate members the leading importers of raw cotton as well as chief
exporters of cotton goods. The Japan Cotton Spinners' Association had made
an agreement with the Associetion of Raw Cotton Dealers which provided
that the latter would sell only to members of the Japan Cotton Spinners'
Association. Even freight rebates were arranged,-for members of the
Japan Cotton Spinners' Association only. Before the mr this association
controlled 98 percent of Japanese cotton spinning capacity and about 40
percent of all cotton weaving capacity in Japan.
The organization of the silk industry followed a different pattern
from that of cotton and other branches of the industry because of certain
special characteristics of Japanese silk production. Unlike all other
branches of the Japanese textile industry, silk depended on a locally
produced raw material and not on imports. Millions of farm households
produced the cocoons for silk and suffered with each decline in the price
of silk. Price fluctuations were particularly acute in Japan because
her silk was even more dependent on the export market than her other
textile products Silk was exported for the most part in its raw state
and not as woven goods. Furthermore, silk reeling, unlike cotton, woolen
and rayon spinning, was conducted mostly on a small scale by thousands of
separate filatures.

efforts, especially during the depression in the thirties which had a very
severe impact on the silk industry and Japan's agricultural districts.

II. WarPeriod
While basicly the Japanese textile industry, being & peace industry
had to be scaled down during the war, it has never-the-less played an
important part in Japan's war effort. It's role change with the differest
phases of the war. Until Pearl "arbor the chief role of the tertile
industry was to provide the Treasury with the maximum amount of foreign
exchange and thus enable Japan to buy strategio war materials on the
open market. Since Pearl arbor the textile industry had as its
positive funotion the provisioning of the Japanese war machine with
an adequate supply of textiles, providing a minimm amount of textile
goods for the Japanese civilian population, and even producing a amall
amount of textiles to be used as incentive goods in obtaining essential
raw materials from the occupied areas. With the increasing tempo of
the war the Japanese textile industry became more and more important
in its negative function of releasing materials, equipment, labor, and
plant facilities for the use of major war industries, aircraft, mmchinery
and chemical production. These various aspects of the Japanese textile
industry's contribution to the war effort raised special problems and
necessitated the imposition of the new and far-reaching regulations on the
industry.
I.
Maximising Foreign Exchange
In order to gear the Japanese textile industry to the goal of
maximizing foreign exchange it was necessary not only to stimulate
exports but even more important to reduce the domestic consumption
14-000 207.00

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.97)
of textiles mmde from imported tertile rew materials, especially the
more expensive and serviceeble tertile rew materials, - cotton and wool.
1n l957 the japanese sovernment attempted to direct the flow of imported
raw materials iato the produstion of goods destined for erport by licensing
all tertile raw materiel importers and by decrees compelling manufacturers
of tertiles for the home market to mix a certain pereentage (originally
30 percent) of staple fiber yarn with both cotton and woolen goods. The
percentage of admixture of staple fiber yarn wms later inoreased until
finelly no cotton or wool could be used in tertiles for the Japanese
civilian population. Restrictions were even put on the amount of
imported rayon pulp which could be made up into tertiles for the home
market. Only silk remained unregulated and the government tried to
stinulate the domestic utilization of silk by setting up in September
1941 a Silk Utilization Company to assist the industry in converting
from the production of fine counts for luxury goods to coarse counts for
goods of greater strength and durability. The shortage of tertiles for
home consumption made necessary the centrelized control of textile dis-
tribution and the regulation of textile prices by the Minister of Commeroe
and Industry. Thus even before Pearl Harbor the Japanese textile industry
was taking on the chief characteristics of a controlled war economy.
The initial control measures proved to be ineffective in the face
of the gradual price rise at home which made it more profitable to sell
tertiles on the Japanese market then abroad. As a result a new method
of control was put into operation in the summer of l938, the so-called

The effects on the Japanese textile industry of this drive to expand
exports by means of the link system were manifold. Export associations were
strengthened. Manufacturers who produced tertiles primarily for erport, i.e.
the large firms, were able to make good profits while smell and medium sised
firms catering to the home market suffered increasing financial difficulties.
Large firms were fabored over those small and medium sised firms which did

limited order there were, ostensibly because it was easier to trace the flow
of imported raw materials in the large plants as there were fewer units to
police. Cartels and associations were used to administer the program. For

a. Increasing Textile Raw Material Production
Pearl Harbor changed the Japanese tertile pioture overnight as

b. Need for Iaposing Far Reaching Controls on the Industry
Despite some increase in tertile raw material production within
the empire, the total supply of such raw materials was still far short
of the amount needed to meet the growing requirements of the armed foroes
for uniforms and other textile equipment such as parachutes, as well as
the demands of the civilian population. It was therefore necessary to
introduce centralised controls to allocate the limited supply among
all consming groups on a priority basis and to ration the limited
supply set aside for civilian consumption.
Not only the quantity but the quality of textiles produoed in
Japan had to be regulated. To supply essential needs only, textile
products were standardised and simplified and the more elaborate types
requiring special pattern and dyeing processes were strictly curtailed
if not abolished. Certain types of textiles were allowed, and others
proscribed for different consuming groups, - cotton and wool went exclusive-
ly to the army, rayon and staple fiber to the civilians. Silk, hemp and
even staple fiber had to be mixed with the dwindling supplies of cotton
and wool to provide the army's needs. Likewise some silk and hemp were
mixed with the rayon and staple fiber to strengthen the textiles to be
consumed by the civilian population.
This program required the imposition of an infinite mumber
of rules and regulations on the industry as well as an elaborate con-
trol structure to supervise and police all phases of textile pro-
duction and distribution. The mechanism for control of distribution
to the ultimate consumer are dealt with in the Quide on Price Control
and Rationing of Consumer Goods Other than Food. The chief means of

d. The Fabrio Control Society
While the tertile control structure as established in 1942 appeared

rival units competing with each other for materials and orders.

material lines of no value and a positive hindrance.

diffioult to exercise control over the component parts because of their
great dissimilarity. Constituent organisations and firms, often at
cross purposes to each other found it hard to agree on & president.

5. Releasing Resources for the War Industries.
With the mounting urgency to use every industrial resource in Japan in
war production, a program was adopted in l945 for systematic absorptiom ofall
idle mdustrial resources into war industries. According to the 194s Adjustmest
Plan all Japanese industry was to be divided into three categories.
1. Important peace-time industries with excess plant, labor end equip-
ment which could be used by heavy industry and strictly war industries.
2. Heavy industries and war industries which should be expanded when
the resources in group one were made available to them.
3. Miscellaneous small scale industries-such as food processing and
retail trade, i.e., industries without such equipment or plant fecilities for
coiversion purposes but absorbing the labor of a great many people who could
be released from their present occupations if these trades were sufficiently
curtailed and thereby augment the labor foroe available to industries in
group two.
Since textile production had been Japan's chief industry in peace-time,
it became her major source of idle plant and equipment for war conversion
purposes and scrap metal. This program necessitated a further reduction in
textile production, concentration of what tertile production was to be per-
mitted in a few pilot plants, further standardization and simplification of
tertile products for civilian consumption, and, in short, the complete
rationalisation of the industry, a culmination of the rationalisation pro-
cess which had been taking place for many years. While all branches of the
tertile industry were arfected by this drastic program, its impact on mall

gdants.

of post-war textile profits from their textile plants in Japan proper and
throughout the empire by buying up at nominal prioes the medium sised and small
spinning firms, idle for lack of raw material. The govermmest accelerated the

to war industries, the plant to be used and the equipment to be melted dowm into
serap.
According to the 1943 Adjustment Plan, the plant and equipment of the textile
industry were divided into three groups. Plants in group eme were designated
to remain in operation; plants in group two were designated to remain in reserve
as textile plants in case of need (the owners of the plants to be assisted by e
mutual aid fund based on past earning records and subscribed to by firms remain-
ing in operation); and plants in group three to be converted to war produetion
and their textile equipment scrapped. The plant and equipment in group three
was to be bought up by the Japanese government through two organizations set
up to assess the value of the confiscated properties and finance their purchase
from their present owmers and resale to firms engaged in war production, -the
People's Rehabilitation Bank to bring out amall firms and the Industrial Equip-
ment Management Corporation to handle medium and large firms. Large funds were
appropriated to finance this extensive property transfer and arrangements
made to reduce the possibility of inflation by paying a certain percentage
of the assessed value in government bonds.
14-086 P58b

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.98)
Though additional spinning plants were converted to war produstiom as
a remalt of the Ad fustment Plant, the chief impact of the 1945 progrem
on the spinning branch of the tertile industry was the purehase by the
goversment of idle spindleage to be melted down into serap. Reports
indicate considerable haggling over the assessed valuation of the liquidated
textile plants and equipment. In the end the sovernment purohased vast
quantities of cotton psinning equipment. It is estimated that at the
end of the war only 2,700,000 of Japan's original 13,000,000 ootton spindles
remained. The sale of idle tertile equipment to the sovernment provided the
large textile firms with considerable liquid assets which they proceeded to
invest in a wide variety of industries both in Japan and throughout the
opiro.
In the case of medium and small textile establishments which had been
engaged in producing and finishing tertile fabrics, the effeot of the
Adjustment Program was wholesale liquidation of firms. Though the owmers
of these shops received sone compensation it is highly unlikely that they
received a sufficient amount to be able to invest in some aspect of the
war production program as independent firms. Silk reeling, largely a
small scale operation, was also reorganized in the summer of l943. A huge
new company, the semi-official Raw Silk Company, was set up to absorb the
thousands of independent silk reeling units. The company dominated by
the leading silk firms, succeeded in absorbing practically the entire
raw silk industry, with but a few insignificent exceptions.
The combined effect of the shock of Pearl Harbor on the Japenese
textile industry plus the deliberate policy of the government acting
through the pontrol mocieties was a complete transformation of the structure
of the industry. Instead of the different branches of the spinning industry

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.96)
being composed of a few giant firms plus several dosen mdeium sised firms, caly
the giants remained. The mumber of independent tertile establishments in
weaving and finishing was greatly reduded with the very small shops virtually
wiped out. The number of tertile firms fell from over 28,000 in 1937 (plus more
than l00,000 amall shops enploying less than five workers) to an estimated
7,000 in 1944. This proccess of attrition against small units continued even
futher with new regulations imposed during l944 prohibiting firms running less"
than a stipulated number of mechines from remaining in tertile produetion. The
minimum was fixed at 300 woolen looms, 300 cotton-staple fiber mmchines, over
l00 artificial silk and hemp machines, etc. Sinoe 1944 the mumber of tertile
firms has continued to shrink.

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.98)
C - RECOMMENDATIONS
I. Role of the Textile Industry in Post-Surrender Japan
Resumption of textile production in Japan is of immediate
importance in order tos-
1. Provide Japanese civilians with an essential mini-
mum of clothing and household linen requirements.
2. Provide Japan with a means of acquiring foreign ex-
change with which Japan can buy approved essential
imports.
3. Provide Japan with an eventual source for reparations
payment in kind (either finished goods or equipment)
to the former victims of Japanese aggression.
II. Present State of the Textile Industry
A large proportion of the Japanese textile industry has disap-
peared. Textile plants have been used for war production. Textile
equipment has been shipped overseas, melted down into scrap, or converted
to heavy industry and war production.
III. Measures Necessary to Revive the Textile Industry
To enable Japan to produce the maximun amount of textiles the
following steps should be takens-
1. Japan should be allowed to import the necessary raw
materials,-salt and if necessary timber and pulp
for her synthetic fiber industry, cotton, wool and
waste silk. The importation of some textile machin-
ery may be advisable.
10-006 766 0

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.98)
50
2. The Japanese textile machinery industry which was
converted to war production early in the war should
be reconverted as soon as possible to the production
of textile machinery. Depending upon the facilities
at hand and the possibility of conversion of war in-
dustry to the production of textile machinery, soals
should be set bearing in mind the pre-war capacity
of the industry and the tremendous back-log of textile
machinery needs.
3. Textile mills and rayon pulp mills should be re-
paired wherever possible, i.e., where damage has not
been irreparable.
4. Textile equipment which has been converted to other
uses should be reconverted back to the textile in-
dustry wherever reconversion is feasible.
i. To carry out the above measures it will be neces-
sary to make an extensive inventory of the pre-war
textile plants and equipment to ascertain how much
of it is intact, - in use, idle or stored away, -
how much of it has been damaged but can be repaired,
and how much of it was converted to war production
in such a way that it can be reconverted back to
textile production. Technical experts in various
branches of the textile industry will be needed to
evaluate the possibilities under 4.
14-008 P66 

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.98)
Iv. Prodnotion Goal
The production goal in tertiles should be the meximem. smount
possible that is consistent with the findings of the above mentdomed
inventory. There should be provision for increasing the soal
as more equipment is made available to the industry.
v. Distribution of the Output
It is recommended that of this total textile production approm-
imately 187,000 tons of synthetic fiber be allocated to mest the
minimum needs of the Japanese civilian population for the first year
of operation of the industry after the war. This figure represents
the amount of textiles allocated to the Japanese civilian population
during 1943. Synthetic fiber rather than cotton is proposed heommse
of the greater cost in foreign exchange of cotton goods as opposed
to synthetic fiber yarn even if all the pulp had to be imported (whict
is not the case). All production above this minimm should be used
to clothe the victims of Japanese aggression in the Orient or to
supply Japan with foreign exchange (depending on Allied polioy on
the time, extent and nature of reparations payments). In succeeding
years textile consumption in Japan as well as throughout the Orient
would inorease and more and more cotton would be consumed in Japan.
VI. War-time Controls
All laws establishing and implementing the war-time control
structure in the Japanese textile industry and particularly the
Major Industries Organisations Ordinance setting up control asso-
ciations and control societies should be abrogated. The whole control
structure which aimed primarily at restricting and limiting the in-
14-000P070

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.97)
dastry should be abolisbed. Sach controls as may be needed to
facilitate the expansion of textile production and the regalstion of
tarxtile distribution should not be administered through a costrel
structure dominated by a few large textile firms intent on preserv-
ing their stranglehold on the industry.
VII. Textile Control Commission
A Textile Control Commission should be established to supervise
the administration of the textile program as authorised by Military
Government. This Commission should be concerned not only with the
quantitative aspects of the program,-inventory of equipment, ex-
pansion of textile facilities, imports, exports and the distribution
of the total product,-but also with the basic reorganisation of the
Japanese textile industry to insure that in future it serve the
Japanese people rather than Japan's industrial and military war lords.
VIII. Reorganization of the Japanese Textile Industry
The Japanese textile iidustry has long been dominated by the
large cotton spinning firms closely tied to the leading Zaibatsu's
industrial and financial empires. The grip of these firms on the
industry was greatly increased as a result of the war which enabled
the giant tertile firms to concentrate practically all the remaining
textile capacity in their own hands. These firms dominate not only
the cotton industry, but also woolen and worsted production, the
synthetic fiber industry, the spinning of waste silk and the hemp-
indmstry.
To re-establish free private enterprise in the Japanese
textile industry and to wrest control of the industry from those who
1-8 P8 

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.98)
backed and pronoted Japan's war of aggression, the management of the
giant cotton spinning firms should be.divorced from the Zaibatsu
and subjected to control by the Textile Control Commission. Second-
ly, positive steps to insure the re-emergence of independent textile
firms should be taken. For medium sized firms to revive and re-enter
the industry, provision will have to be made to enable them to re-
purchase existing equipment at the low prices at which they were
forced to sell to the large firms. This will require a liberal
credit policy designed to favor medium and mall firms whether old
firms or new-comers. Such firms should have first call on new equip
ment as it comes on the market and should receive preferential treat-
ment with respect to textile raw materials which Military Government
allows the Japanese to import into Japan.
Once medium sised firms have been re-established it will be
necessary to safeguard their independence from the giant textile
firms by all possible means. The chief means will be the organisa-
tion of genuine cooperative producers' associations which are not
dominated by the textile giants or outside interests and which are
powerful enough to obtain credit and other facilities on advantagoous
terms. To safeguard the revived kogyo kumiai, it will be necessary
to overhaul the body of statutary laws regulating business organisa-
tions with a view to strengthening the kogyo kumiai form of organi-
zation found among small and medium sise firms. The type of close
knit cartel which the large textile firms have organised and
dominated and whose main function has been the restriction of pro.
duction should be dissolted and all restrictive deals declared
invalid.

Regulatione of the Woolen Goode Contral
Socisty
General Regulations
1)
The purpose of this society shall be to support
the national policy of controlling all woolen
goods business in order to stremgthem the
national economic system.
2)
This society shall be called the Woolen Goods
Control Society and shall be based on the Degree
of Essentiel Industry.
3)
The headquarters of this society shall be locsted
in Tokyo and branch offices shall be establisbed in
other cities.
4)
The announcement of this society shall be reveeled
in the gasette.
II. "embers
5)
This society shall be csmposed of all woolen
business firms which have been approved by the
Minister of Commerce.
III. Work and its execution
6)
In order to fulfil the aim of regulation No. 1
the following work shall be done by memberss
a) To aid the government in
acqufring material, capital
and labor for the increase of
wool production.
b) To direct and control the produotion
and distribution of the woolen industry.

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
c) To control the supply of capital
material and labor for the woolen
industry
d) To fix the price for woolen goods
•) To promote planning the woolen
industry
f) To improve the skill and to increase
the efficiency of the woolen industry
g) To make surveys and do research con-
cerning the woolen industry.
h) Besides the above items, other necessary
works for the benefit of this society
will be required.
7) Control regulations of this society shall be
recognised by the Minister of Commerce
The president shall consult with the counselors
in order to formulate control regulations.
8) Essential affairs of this society shall be
executed by the president.
IV. Officers
9) This society shall have one president, one
chief director and a certain mumber of directors,
superintendents and counselors.
10) The president shall be recommended through a
member of the Civil Service Committee and shall
be appointed by the inistry of Commerce.
The chief director and directors shall have
good knowledge of and experience in the woolen
industry and their appointments shall be
approved by the Minister of Comerce.
Counselors and superintendent should have
experience in the woolen industry and their
appointments shall be approved by the president.
11) The appointments of the president, the chief
director and the directors shall be for three years
each and of the superintendents and counselors,two years each.
If the president wishes to resign during his inoumbency his
resignation shall be approved by the Minister of Commerce.
14-080 P188 

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
12) The president, the chief director and directors
cannot engage in other business excspt with the
approval of the Minister of Commerce.
12) The president shall direct and control all
essential affairs of the woolen industry and
other activities related to this society.
The chief director and directors shallassist
the president in all works and act for him in
his absence.
Superintendents shall examine and mudit the
financial status of this society.
Counselors shall answer and give opinions to,
the president upon request.
v. Conferences
14) There are two kinds of conferences, namely:
a) general conference
b) counselors conferences.
The general conference shall be composed of all
members of this society while the counselors
conference shall be composed of only the
counselors of this society.
15) There are also two kinds of general con-
ferences namely:
a) ordinary conference
b) emergency conferences.
The ordinary conference shall be held in
May of each year, while an emergency conference
may be called whenever it is needed. Con-
ferences shall be called by the president.
16) The president shall consult all members
at the conference concerning any change in
the regulations or the financial budget of
the society.
17) At the anmual ordinary confernece the
president shall report on all affairs and
conditions and the superintendents shall
report on the financial statements of the
society.
14-000 PL86 N

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
18) A counselors conference may be called
at the will of the president. The
president shall be the chairman of the
counselor's conference.
VI. Bureau of General Affairs
19) A Bureau of General Affairs shall be set
up to handle all general affairs of this
society.
20) The chief director shall be the head of
the Bureau of General Affairs and shail
be under the direction and supervision
of the president.
21) Several départments shall be under the
Bureau of Affairs and shall be headed
up by directors.
VII. Accounting
22) The fiscal calendar of this society shall
be from Aprilll of one year to Marsh 31 of
the following year.
23) All members shall pay a membership fee.
The membership fee and the method of
collecting it shall be determined by
the president. He shall consult members
at the ordinary conference and adopt member's
opinion.
VIII. Dissolution and Liquidation
24) This society may be dissolved by the order
of the Minister of Commerce.
25) The liquidator shall be selected by the
court.
26) The liquidator shall have full authority to
represent this society.
27) The liquidator shall determine the method
of liquidating and handling property which
shall be approved by the court.

regulstions of this society shall be
fined not more than 5,000 yen.
29) A member who violated the control
regulations of this society shall be fined
not more than 10,000 yen.

Dear Mr. Martin:
89
I wish to thank you for your telegram of December 12th advising me of
the address which can be used to reach us while we are away on the textile mission.
4
.655/12-1945
I received word Monday from Col. Carew that the mission will not be ord-
ered out until the middle of next week, and have notified Mr. Comer accordingly.
Col. Carew is notifying the other members.
This leaves a few extra days to enable me to receive additional reports
and information which will be of value to our mission. For instance, I spent the
last week-end studying the special reports of the Tariff Commission on the recent
industry in Japan and found reference to salt and caustic soda. These reports
have just been sent to me upon my request and they will be of value in determing
questions of raw material for viscose manufacturing.
In that connection I am somewhat disturbed by the report that Ambassador
Pawley is considering depriving Japan of its chemical industry including sulphurio
acid. I suggest that he be advised promptly of the importance of sulphuric acid in
the manufacture of viscose rayon. The Japanese rayon industry has been practically
C
independent as to raw materials, except pulp, but if they were deprived of the
S
necessary acid, the plants could not operate. In view of the fact that transporta-
tion of the acid is physically hazardous and economically unsound, the amount of
acid that will be needed is, of course, one of the questions to be determined by
m
our mission.
In addition to government reports I am collecting information on the
indusry in Japan from various private sources. I understand that detailed infor-
mation on every rayon plant in Japan and its location on the map was given ta:G 2
or Oss. Mr. Simmons tells me that these maps and this information will be made a
vailable to me.

the plants, we will go our respective ways since the other members ef

Dear r. Thorp:
Re: JK
I have your thoughtful letter of January 5th.
I want to tell you that it was with a great deal
11
of pride that I received an invitation from Mr. Martin to be
a member of his group to go to Japan. And then it was with
more regret that I had to withdrai! my name as a prospective
member. As I told ir. Martin, I had comnitments in February
that had to be met, so when the time of the departure was ex-
tended, and also the possible length of the trip extended, it
was apparent that I woui: run into conflicts with these en-
gagements. Then, too, as the year went out the strikes in the
Labor situation gave us all some concern and 1 felt I had just
rather be around the place now than to be away from home, es-
pecially since my job is on the ground at home work.
Please, though, figure that my brother Donald and I
S/LE
are keenly interested in tnis situation and either one, or
both of us, will be happy to discuss any phase of this situa-
tion that you would care to bring to our attention.
Please express my highest regards to Mr. Will Ciayton.
I have no fear of flatterin: him when I say I think he is one
of the smartest, if not the smartest, man in the United States.
JAN 24 1946
Yours very truly,
AVONDALE MILLS

My dear Mr. Byrnes:
894.655/2-1346
I am in receipt of a letter,
dated February 7, 1946, from the Honorable Clinton P.
Anderson, Secretary of Agriculture, setting forth the terms
of an agreement to be entered into between the Department
of State, the War Department, Commodity Credit Corporation
and U.S. Commercial Company under which raw cotton owned by
Commodity Credit Corporation is to be shipped to Japan and
manufactured into cotton textiles.
The agreement is satisfactory
to the U.S. Commercial Company and, in accordance with the
suggestion of the Secretary of Agriculture, I send you here-
with a copy of his letter with our acceptance noted. Similar
copies are being sent to the Secretary of War and the
Secretary of Agriculture.

Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Martin:
Rayon Definitions
Before I went on the Textile Mission to Japan I noted that re-
ports on the rayon industry in Japan in various branches of the Government,
including F.E.A., Tariff Commission, Department of Commerce, as well as the
State Department, contained misuses of rayon nomenclature. When I reached
Japan, it became apparent that the origin of such misuse was the Japanese
industry and accordingly I took steps to try to correct it in that industry.
I also left at SCAP in three different offices, the Textile Branch,
the Economic Research Section, and the Export-Import Section, a list of do-
finitions which might help get the subject back on the beam. I am enclosing
①
a copy of that list for your information and I would appreciate your circu-
lating it in all offices of the State Department where it might do some good.
I am writing the Tariff Commission and the Department of Comi rge to the same
effect.
The principal misuse is the Japanese custom of referring to rayon
yarn as rayon and to rayon staple as staple fiber. They divide the industry,
therefore, into rayon and staple fiber as though they were quite different
products, whereas rayon staple is only one of the rayon products, while yarn
is another. This misuse is more than academic, but leads to misunderstanding
of reports and statistics. One can study a report of the reyon industry of
Japan at some length before discovering that it is dealin unly with yarn and
not with staple.
On the pther hand, the authority for rayon nomenclature in the United
States is the Américan Society for Testing Materials and definitions are given
in their annual book prepared by Committee D - 13 on textile materials.
AY 13 1946
Sincerely yours,
FILED
HuRars
5
913
1946
enclosure
H. Wickliffe Rose

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.98)
REPORT OF TEXTILE HISSION TO PAE
The Textile Mission, sponsored by State and War Depts. and
posed or representatives of China, India, U.K., and U.S., appral
Japan's ability to produce textile products in 1946-47 with parti
reference to her ability to relieve serlous world shortage by making
available cotton textile products for export after meeting domestio
requirements.
It was early apparent that the magnitude of bomb damage had not
been under-estimated and the extent of the reduction in machinery
through scrapping already reported had not been exaggerated.
SUMMARY:
1. Maximum output of cotton textile products in l946 1s sufficient
to absorb the import of 225,000 short tons of cotton, while an aoourate
appaisal of cotton textile position in 1947 will depend upon results
of l946 estimates. It 1s assumed that cotton shipments including
supplies in 1947 will continue sufficient to build up a stock equal
to at least two months' consumption
2. The rayon yarn capacity 1s about equal to Japan's averago
domestic consumption from 1933-38 and if produced and absorbed could
release substantial amounts of raw silk.
3. Estimates of reconversion and production in woolen and worsted
industry for 1946 are more uncertain than those made of cotton position
Mixture cloths made from stocks on hand are unlikely to be useful for
export since lo0% wool and worsted cloths have the bulk of such demand.
Both the U.S. and the U.K. tend to develop wool and rayon staple
mixture cloths. Any wool import program, as yet undetermined, should
be considered along with that for other fibers.
4. At the end of February incomplete records show that Japan had
stocks of 575 million square yards of cloth and materials sufficient
to produce an additional 425 million square yards of oloth. What
portion of this will be available for export depends on survey made of
clothing being worn by Japanese today. Compared with other oountries
there is no direct evidence of undue clothing shortage.
5. Problem of manufacturing new textile machinery 1s pomplicated
by problems of reparations and removal of war potential. Ufti
existing and repaired spindles in l947 are working to capacfty nd until
level of production is finally determined, Mission recommenda tat
production of new machinery be controlled in the strictest rssIble
8
manner.
6. Shortage of mill labor is an immediate problem facing textile
industry. Due to lack of food most of the mill workers, who are girls,
prefer to remain in the country where food is more plentiful.
31
CS/H
JA:HKullgren/pm
HOB
cat___ek

6. A problom facing the Japanese textile industry is the availability
of labor. The pr sont shortage is due directly to the shortage of the food
which the mills make available to their workers under the Japanese Government's
daily rice ration. Most of the mill workers, who sro girls, prefer to ramain
in rural areas where fooi is plentiful. Mill owners have been forced to depend
upon the black market to secure additional food for their workers.

I. JAPAN'S TEXTILE CAPACITY
A. Cotton Toxtiles
From the figures available to the Mission prior to its deperture
from Washington, it was apparent that a very big change had ocourred in the
capacity of the J'spanese textile industry. The Mission felt that ite first
obligation would have to be a verification of the statistics that had been
reported to Washington. Upon our arrival in Tokyo all operating companies
were requested to furnish full data as to pre-war and present tertile oquip-
ment, showing reduction through scrapping, bomb damage, and transfer of
equipment within Japan snd overseas. (See Table V ). A number of wide mill
surveys were then mede, cxtending over several weeks, and covering the main
centers of pr duction. These visits included all sections of the industry
and both damaged and destr oyed factories. Although these surveys were made
with the cooperation of SCAP and the Japanese textile industry, they were
largely unplanned snd served as valuable spot checks.
It was early apparent that the magnitude of bomb lamage had not been
under-est imated and tho extent of the reduction in machinery through scrap-
ping already reported, hai not been exaggerated.) Transfer of equipment
overseas, however, was rclatively smail.
1. Sninning

spindles the 1ndustry had been reduced as of February 1946 to .2,150,000 in-
stalled spindles of which 1,115,000 were operable.
These opindles are now concentrated in only ten companies
as a result of amalgamet:ons effected during the war. A small additional
capacity of 242,000 epindles exists, owned by rayon companies, about half of
which are suitable for ra on staple spinning only. (See Part I. B).

can be made only by the inetellation of new machinery. The estimates for

ditions of new machine y. This assumes that the manufacturing capacity of the
textile machinery industry will be fully occupied until thc fourth quarter of
1946 in repairing damaged machinery. As a result of the Mission's survey
of many bombed factories, we believe that it is impossible to forecast
accurately the fu'l ex ent to which it will ultimately pay spinners to pepair
damaged machinery as au alternative to replacement by new machinery. Thus,
it is possible for there to be some installation of new machinery within our
totals, without affecting the figures of estimated yarn production. (Capacity
of textile machinery manfacturers ie discussed in Part II of this report).

cotton, it was found that the use from year to year of cottons of vary

2. Weeving
As a result or the wide divcrsification of ownership of power
looms in Japan, it is much more difficult to summarize accurately the present
position of the weaving in lustry. The looms of the cotton mills of the ten
operating companics are entirely automatic, and of widths suited for the
manufacture of 27" cloth and over, being installed as integrated units of

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.98)
7
spinning mills. The past, pwesent, and projected status of
summarised in Table V, was declared to the Miosdon by these oe
at the same time as their spindleage. Reduction from prewar levele
place through scrapping, bombing, and transfers similar to those whie
fected spindles. Out of a total of 22,651 installed looms in the spimnos
these estimated figures sre forecast to increase to a total in January 1947
of 40,030 instellcd loons, of which an estimated 30,383 would be operable.
(See Table J.V).
Independent weavers have always held a larger total of power
looms ihen the spinning companies, although such looms are almost entirely
non-automatic. Scme were concentrated in relatively large units but in pro-
war years there were thousands of small wcaving plants averaging under ten
looms each. A proportion of these looms were very narrow being suitable for
the wcaving of 18" cloths and under. With restrictions on production re-
sulting from the war, mery of these plants disappeared as a result of a
voluntary scrapping progran inatituted by the Government along with various
concentration schemes. Nevertheless the independent weaters have reported
that even today they number 2,500 owning approximately 68,500 operable wide
looms and 28,500 narrow looms. It is difficult to assess these figures owing
to their interchangesbility with fibres other than cotton. The Jooms of
the spinning compenies operate on two shifts, working 8) hours cach, while
customary working hours of non-automatic weaving sheds are only ono shift of
10 hours per day. It hac been possible for the Mission to visit only a few
factories belonging to the independent weavers but it is apparent that the
damage through scrapping and air raids has not been on the same scale as in
the spinning mills. The weavers have declared damaged wide looms totaling
6,128 but we are unccrtain what percentage of these may eventually be added
to the 68,500 declared o be operable.

During the war, as shown in Table X, the rayon industsy
physical capacity was reduced by scrapping and bombings similar to 
ions in the cotton, woolen, and worsted industries. No transfer of equly
ment to Japanese-occupied territories in Asia seeme to have occurred durims
the war.
The present actual capacity of machinery in place of 306 million
pounds is not a siable figure. Since the war ended, somo mills have been
busily engaged in ro-insalling some of their stored or reserve equipment,
the capacity of which totals 47 million pounds. This reserve added to
machinery in place gives a total. potential productive capacity of 354 mil-
lion pounde.
A number of companies are planning to roplace. units which
have becn scrapped. However, a eevere shortege of rew materials and lack
of repair and rehabilitation machinery makes any increase in actual capacity
quite irrolovart
Because of cuch shortages of materials it is entiwated by the
industry that consideral:y less than 100 million pounds of rayon will be
produced in 1946. The extent to which the presont nominal production rate
can be increascd towar? the potantial capacity dependa upon the avail-
ability of materials such as coal end caustic soda and upon machine parts
and equipmant to restore the reserve capacity. The industry estimates
that the potential capaity limit of 354 million pounds will not be reached
by the end of 1947.

cated to industrial usen. The following is the 1945 allocation by percent
of rayon consumpticn:

Rayon yarn offers no problem since the maximom pote

The present guality of rayon in moss plants visited by the
Mission is be"ow normal etendards. Pulp is spotty and of irregular content.
No chlorine is used for leaching, caustic soda contains calciun when made
from soda ash, and viacoas is gonerally of poor spinning gunlity. Tenstle
strength is low, staple io irregular, cclor is dark, and the fibre in many
mills contains the usual flaws resulting from bad viscose and spinning con-
ditions. The present qualty of reyon, particularly staple, has brought
it into low repute by cotton spinners and consuasrs. Rayon yarn has enjoyed
a better reputaticn. Pi:ing tha war high tenacity yara was produced for
tire cord and for unifoius and work clothing. (6 miliicn pounds in 1943;
7 million in 194:; and 2 willion in 1945). Tino present equipment for high
tonacity ie not opsrating bocause of the coal shortage.

excellent spinning gueiity end makes excellent reyon fabrics. As production
increases, quallty wili voubtedly improve. The quality of rayon, there-

2. Availability_of Ray Matariela.
Soon after its arrival in Japan, it beceme apparent to the
Mission from its preliminary surveys that the rayon industry would close
within a matter of montts if salt were not imported into Japan and made
available for caustic soda production. Japan has sufficient caustic soda
cepacity , providing it is not seriously reduced by an Allied reperations
progrem, to meet tha regairemonts of the rayon indusiry if the salt is made
available. It was alco indicatcs by the Mission that the ssopping and
starting of a rayon plant takes time, and that a complete stoppago of
operating plants would io wasteful. The Mission understands that attention
is being givan to a program for salt imports, with April tentatively
scheduled as the beginning of such imports, but the amount to be made avail-
able f r the rayon indua!ry is uncertain. Meauvhile, since nearly all rayon
plants are located on the seacoast, many of them are now operating, or plan
to operate, salt farms polucing salt by solar evaporation of seawater.
Some rayon plants aro  so crystallizing brine by electric heat within the
rayon plants. These Csvices do not contribute more then a small fraction
of the rayon industry's salt roquirements.
Anothor raw nateriel shortage of the rayon industry is coal.
Although the full reguirements of the rayon industry for coal have not yet
been met, it is auticipated that coal output will increase in future months.
In the meantime, to mininizo the use of coal, a number of rayon plants are
installing electrical quiprent to utilize Japan's hydroelectric production
(See Part II, D).

4. Other Synthetic Fibres.
There are 'trs units with capecities for producing synthetio
fibres other than rayon in Japan.
The Shiga Plant of Toyo Rayon Kk ie producing monofil and film
from a polyanida rosin. The yarn from this resin is used for fishing lines.
Capecity of the piant s 0 metric tons a month.
The Tokushima Plant of Teikoku Seni YK produces protein from
soy bean, which is bleneed with viscose to make a cellulcse-protein rayon
staple. The capacity cf protein production is 500 kilograms daily.
In aidition to these two synthetic fibre factories, the Todogawa
plant of Kanegaiichi Moeyo XL had a factory for producing a polyvinal
alcohol fibre at a capacity of 500 kilograms daily. Its machinery was
scrapped during the war.
