C. Yoolens and Wozsteds
The concentration of the Japanese worsted industry in the hands of a
paratively few conpanies with integrated operations nakes it similar in this
respect to the caton spinning industry. However, the woolen industry is
tharacterized by a gruat range in the capacity of mills fron very large to mall
units and by a diversification of owmership. The present 454 available cards
in the woolen industry are hcld by 52 mills: an average of 8.7 cards per mill
with some mills having as faw as one or two cards. In general, worsted companies
have their own dysing and finishing facilities, but the woolen industry makes
use of sone independentdyers and finishers although it deals mainly with fibres
in dyed form.
Owing to this lack of concentration in the woolen industry and the absence
of a strong woolen and worsted association, the Mission has found it more
difficult to collect accurate data than in the cotton textile field. The
woolen and worsted industries were requested to submit information on their
pre-war, present, and projocted cepacity sinilar to that provided by the
cotton industry. Menbers of the Mission made a number of visits to woolen
and worsted mills which provided valuable spot checks on the information
presented.
1. Spinnin
The Japanese woolen industry did not suffer during the war from
bombing or government sorapping orders to the sane extent as either the cotton,
rayon or worsted industries. This was due to the location of nost woolen
mills away fron urban target areas and to the essential need for the woolen
industry to provide clothing and blankos for the Japanese arned forces. As
shown in Table XIv, the naber of woolen cards was reduced from 765 in 1939
to 454 in February 1945 (of which only 251 of the latter were in place), a

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
reduction of about 40 per cent. The number of worsted spindles, whse
from 1,130,000 in 1935 to 1,630,000 in 1939, was reduced to 380,000 (of
only 325,000 were in place) in February 1946, a loss of almost 78 pes
Of this reduction in worste? spindles almost two-thirds resulted from
However, in comparison to the cotton industry, relatively small mmbers a
woolen cards and worsted spirdles were shipped overseas. It should be noted
that in the transfer of worsied spindles to other fibres as dictated by the
war economy, one company elone (Dai Nippon Boseki Kk) converted almost
55,000 spindles.

During the war, the Jepanese textile industry consumed proportionately
large amounts of silk particularly in mixtures with rayon, wool, and worsted.
The availability of weste or substandard silk and the shortage of other
raw materials was responsible for this policy. With the prospect of new
supplics of raw cotton, it chould no longer be necessary to contimme this
uneconomical prcduction in which the real value of the silk is almost entirely

A. Machinery
In view of widespread interest in the reasons for Japan's increased tex-
tile output, the Mission has paid particular attention to equipment in all
mill visits. While the machinery has been found to be largely of Japanese
make, there was negligible evidenceof any special improvements in cotton
processing machinery over corresponding types in the United States and the
United Kingdom, apart from minor mechanical additions to existing typee of
machinery to suit Japanese working conditions. There was, however, abundant
evidenco of the direct copying in Japan of imported American, German and
British machinery of all types.
A complte study of the textile machinery capacity of Japan is difficult
because the machinery industry comprises not merely a few major companies but
innumerable subcontractors, mostly of a amall general engineering type.

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.98)
3
Relatively little bomb damage was inourred by the few large mechinery com
panies, whose shops were of substantial construction and less subject to
damage by fire. On the other hand there were numerous small subcontrectors
with equipment housed in flimay wooden structures who suffered in greater
. proportion from fire damage. Their capacity represented perhaps up to 30%
of the small parts required in textile machinery.
The Mission has found that approximately 75-85% of new textile machinery
has been produced by the four largest companies, who have depended in the
past on a hundred or more subcontractors for the supply of various parts.
During 1s37, Howa Kogyo KK (Howa Industrial Company) produced 2602 cards.
517 drawing frames, 546 roving frames, 423,000 spindles, 380 twister frames
and 7455 looms. In addition it also produced all the necessary auxiliary
spinning and weaving equipment used in the manufacture of all textile fibres.
Osaka Kikai Seisakusho Kk (Osaka Machinery Manufacturing Company) has reported
their 1937 output as 360 cards, 36 comber units, 144 roving frames, 408,000
spindles and 2400 twister spindles. They were also equipped to poduce all
types of machines and accessories for processing cotton, rayon, wool, silk,
and hard fibres. Osaka Kiko KK (Osaka Machine Company), a smaller firm, had
a 1937 output of 60 cards, 61,600 spindles, 30 combers, 24 roving frames,
and smaller quantities of complementary items. Toyoda Jiko Shokki Seisakusho
KK (Toyoda Automatic Loom Manufacturing Co.) expanded its production to
include a full range of textile machinery for all fibres. From 1927 through
1.541, this firm produced 71,000 1ooms, from 1932 through 1941 over 4,000
cards, and from 1931 through 1942, 2,874,000 spinning spindles.
During the war, the entire facilities of the textile machinery industry
were devoted to the production of munitions. Evidence exists that prior to

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
berasely inoreased as part of Jepes's war preparations te the extemt thee
remaining in the hands of the major machinery producers today is far in em-
oces of what was necessary even in prewar years to satisfy Japan's teztile
requirements.
Reconversion today of some individual works has become involved in
the questions of reparations and the elimination of Japan's war potential.
This problem is further complicated by the desire of other engineering firms.
previously not in the textile field, to take advantage of their increased
equipment and apply for reconversion permits as textile machinery mamufacturers,
thereby hoping to avoid scheduling under a reparations programi
At the same time that the machinery industry was mobilised for munitions
production, spinning and weaving plants were generally considered unessen-
tial, and, under the direction of the armed forces, their equipment was
available for scrapping to meet the shortage of metals. Commencing in 1943,
four scrap drives resulted in a decrease in Japan's cotton spinning and
weaving industry of 7,750,000 spindles and 35,000 looms. Capacities. of
woolen and worsted plants were similarly reduced as has been indicated in
Part I, C.
The scrapping program was based on an organized plan and included all
mschinery complementary to spindles, commencing in the first scrapping with
'steled and inactive spindles. Little attention was paid by the military to
the value of any specialized equipment although compensation was assessed
with some regard to replacement value.

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.97)
the mmchinery taken was broken up on the mill premises by the
Setwabi Midan (Industrial Equipment Corporetion), a government cempesr
formed to deliver the scrap to iron and steel mills through the Metal Serep
Collecting Company. Small weavers were also involved in the scrapping thremg
:. the Kosei Kinko (PeoplesRehabilitation Bank) who induced scrapping by com-
pensation in cash payments. Compensation made by the Sangyo Setsubi Bidan
was generally in the form of non-negotiable bonds, although many accounte are
still outstanding from the last scrapping. Details of the equipment scrapped
in the cotton, rayon, woolen, and worsted industries are shown in Tables VI.
X XVJI, and XIX.
Veri lication of the magnitude of the scrapping program was made through
spot mill visits by the Mission and checked with the Sangyo Setsubi Bidan.
Exact recorciliation of the amounts paid and weights of machinery collected
was not possible in the time at our disposal, but all records exist in the
beoks of the company.
Reduction in the capacity of the Japanese textile industry also resulted
frcm export of machinery to other Japanese-occupied territories, especially
China, as shown in the above-mentioned tables. Although encouraged by the
Government such transfers were made voluntarily, usually to subsidiary
companies of the Japanese spinners, and appear to have been part of a planned
progran.

3mo areas but the Mission has found it difficult to assess the extent to

of ny eerious attempt to bring such machinery into production was seen by

It is significant that these four firms cstimate that they will 
normal capacity during l94s but it is the Mission's belief that this mighs
The repair of damaged equipment alone may lead to this development and the
extent to which bombed machinery has to be restored in the shops of the majer
companies may reduce their estimates of future capacity of new equipment.
The Mission considers that the volume of repair work should occupy the
machinery manufacturers until at least the fourth quarter of 1946. By this
time, the position of supplies of raw material and pig iron may affect their
output of new machinery. Given the necessary quantity of new material to
absorb the stocke of scrap existing in Japan today, there appears to be
little limitation on the ability of the textile machinery industry as a whole
to restore Japan's textile capacity to any level. Account must be taken of
the potential of smaller firme who have specialized in prewar daye in the
duplication of individual textile machines particularly in the woolen, worsted
and rayon industries.
The Mission is not clear as to what extent new textile machinery pro-
duction is currently permitted throughout Japan, apart from control that
arises through the allocations of raw materials. Until the ability of the
cotton spinning industry to reach the production estimates given in this
report is proven, there would appear to be no gain in textile production by
allowing the manufacture of new machinery to add to Japan's existing equip-
ment. Until levels of textile production have been determined, the Mission
is of the opinion that the production of new machinery should be controlled
by SCAP in the strictest possible manner. The Mission is impressed with the
need for an early clarification of the position of the textile manufacturing
industry.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
47
B. Hill storee.and Suppliee
The Hission believes that in the cotton and woolen industries no problem
exists in regard to the availability of mill stores. The normal spares
carried by each mill were not sacrificed during the scrapping program and
from the accumulation of general stores which we have seen in the mills now
in operation we consider that the companies are sufficiently equipped to
reach the capacity outlined in this report. Bome attempt hes been made to
retain spares and small parts and to conserve scrap during the dimmantling
of bombed factories. This procedure will doubtless be extended by the mills
in the cannibalizing of existing equipment.
The type of machinery in rayon plants differs from that of the cotton
and woolen industry. In the scrapping program, little opportunity ocourred
to save equipment spares and the elimination of the individual repair shops
normally attached to each rayon unit has left them badly equipped to maintain
their machinery. To enable the plante to run efficiently special consider-
ation would, therefore, have to be paid to the re-equipment end re-establish-
ment of the machine shops as the factorics return to production.
The problem of lubricating oils has been mentioned to us by both spin-
ners and weavers. Although special oils are normally used, there is no
reason to essume that the industry cannot continue to use the same lubricants
as during the war. The Mission understands that requiremente have already
been given to SCaP and will be dealt with in the overall requirements program
for lubricants.
Starch is the main requirement of the weaving industry in short supply
and the Mission is satisfied that some import is necessary. Prior to the war

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.96)
40
was substituted. But as potatoes are required for food it seoms mecsseury
to make corn starch available for the conversien of cotton to be imported.
The accuracy of the Japanese estimates of total starch required has not been
determined but the types of cloth to be made from cotton to be shipped will
necessitate, in the Mission's opinion, the use of sising materials up to
.approximately 15% of the single yarn warp weight. Half of this amount would
be starch.
Talc and tallow are also usually used in sising mixture as softening
agents, but the use of substitute bils and filling materials is possible.
In India vegetable oils were satisfactorily substituted during the war for
tallow when the latter was not available.
Zinc chloride is necessary as a milder preventative and in view of the
possibility of Japanese exports of gray cloth to monsoon areas it would be
advisable to meet this requirement, which is only 50 metric tons per 200,000
balos of cotton.

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
there is at present a shertage of laber in the Japasese textile
portion of mill labor to agricultural villages as the tertile industry
became curtaild during the war, and from the suspension of the usual rearult-
ing operations of the textile firms.
In the six months after the end of hostilities, the industry has found
it impossible to attract labor to mill employment to the same degree as
before the war. Owing to the Government's daily rice ration of 2.1 go
(0.378 lbs) per person, the workers prefer to remain in rural areas where
they are able to obtain more food than to accept employment in a mill. In
a number of mills, the present labor force has demanded increases in the
daily rice ration to as much as the pre-war lovel of 3.8 so (0.54 lbs.) The
current situation has forced many mill owners to purchase additional quanti-
ties of food from the black market to make up the deficit.
At the end of February, the cotton textile industry reported a labor
supply of about 30,000 workers, 25,000 of whom were females. With increased
mill operations in late spring as a result of raw cotton shipments from the
United States, the Japan Textile Association has formulated a recruitment
program to add between March and September over 136, 00 new workers
(121,000 females) to the labor force of the cotton mills of the ten spinaing
concerns. The Association is depending upon government labor offices and
.'is using advertising for its recruitment program. Although the release of
. students from schools in April will make a large labor force potentially
available, the Association is pessimistic about recruiting more than 80,000
new workers, three-fourthe of whom would be females. However, in the opinion
of the Hission, a total labor force of l00 to 11o thousand workers would be

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.99)
80
sufficient for operation of the cotton textile industry on the basis of
the spindleage and locmage reported. In 1940 when the industry had 7 m
lion operable spindles the total labor force, including labor for weeving
eperations at the cotton mills, was only 142,000 workers.
The industry is not unattractive to Japanese girls as the working
conditions are not onerous. Two shift working is general in all thelarge
mills and labor lawe prohibit the employment of children less than 12 years
of age. Boys under 16 and all women are restricted from night work between
the hours of 11 P.M. and 5 A.M. Approximately three-fourthe of the labor
employed in the industry is female, between the (western) ages of 13 to 17.
They are billeted mainly in dormitories attached to their places of employ-
ment. The living conditions in these dormitories, many of which were visited
by the Mission without prior notice are good. It is roported.by SCar that cotton
mills are being actively unionized and that one company's mills are presently
90 per cent organized. The type of unionization in the cotton textile
industry is not fully known at this time nor is it possible to forecast the
effect of such unionisation on future working conditions. The Mission saw
little evidence of any development during its visits. It is difficult to
visualize the western type of trade union organization among female workers
who join the industry from elementary school and leave to be married be-
tween the ages of 18 to 21.

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.96)
52
D. 2oar_an4 a1
public utility electric power generation for its light and power although a
number of mills have small auxiliary power generating units, most of which
have not been used for many years. Public utility thermal generating plamts
were not damaged to any great extent during the war, and hydroelectric plents,
which provide most of Japan's eleotric power, were not touched. s a result.
sufficient generating capacity exists today to provide for all the light
and power needs of the textile industry.
A number of processes in the textile industry, such as yarn sising.
require heat, which is generated at most mills by coal, although a number of
plants are now converting permanently to electricity for heating. Although
coal was in short supply during the first few months of the occupation,
current coal production is above monthly quotas, and this trend is expected
to continue in the future. Coal allocations by the Japanese Government pro-
vide for coal deliveries in the following order of priority: transportation,
fertilizer, gas and electric power, and textiles. The Mission estimates
that the amount of raw cotton to be consumed in 1946, as shown in Table II.
would require about 155,000 metric tons of coal for complete conversion to
yarn, and finished cloth. The Mission has received the opinion of the appro-
priate authorities in SCaP that the coal requirements of the textile industry
can be met on the basis of the present allocation and priority system and
will not be a critical problem.

U. S. Department of Commerce,

Gentlemen:
Information is requested concerning the possibilities of
trade between India and Japan of cotton textiles, silk goods, art silk
and similar products.
Our Calcutta office has made repeated requests for such types of
goods from the United States.  Due to the limited supplies held in this
country and due to expressions of a desire to build up credits in Japas
with which to purchase essential products and thus partly relieve the
burden on occupation forces, we would be interested to learn in what
manner purchases of Japanese textile goods might be made available to
Indian importers.
We are prepared to offer payment in Sterling. Indian Rupees.
U.S.Dollars or by barter for North Indian cotton and would be pleased
to discuss whatever method of exchange might be acceptable. We understand
that trade negotiations are made at present only through the U.S.Commercial
Company but specific procedure and advice is requested herewith so that we
may be properly guided in subsequent negotiations with the medium through
which we must work.
Tour early attention will be appreciated. Textiles are demper-
ately needed in India and it is our desire to serve many interests by
relaying your comments to our friends abroad.
Yours very truly.
HAYES G. SHIMP. IMC.

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.98)
Delivered before the
Association of Textile
gists. Builders Clmb.
N. Y.. May 1, 1946).
TNE RAYON ONDUSTRY OF JAPAN
By
H Wickliffe Rose
Member. of State Army Text:le Hissicn tc Japan
The Textile Mission to Japan, which recently returned after mak
ing its report in Tokyo, was different from those sent to Germany It was
instructed to learn the capacity to produce textiles in Japan, the needs
of the Japanese people, and the amount of production that might be made
available for export to relieve shortages elsewhere. The Missions to Ger-
many were technical missions to learn about every phase of technology and
machinery in the German industry while this one to Japan was prompted by
the world shortage of textiles particularly cotton and the thought .in
the Textile Committee of the Combined Production and Resources Board, com
posed of representatives of the various Allies, that Japan might contribute
to the world needs
At the same time, in order for the Mission to develop the neces-
sary information on which a report could be based and policy could be formed,
it was necessary for it to learn all it could about the entire textile in
dustry of Japan, including technology. machinery, and machinery manufactur..
ors Thus much of the same type of work as performed by the Mission to
Germany was done in Japan, but the report which was left with General Mac
Arthur to be forwarded to the State Department was quite different in nature.
It will be the purpose here to give some of the aspects of the rayon indus.
try in Japan not required in that report

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.98)
The Textile Mission to Japan was an American mission with fo
observers. The American members were Fred Taylor, Departments of Agrioul
and State, Stanley Nehmer of the Department of State, Frank Rowe of River-
side and Dan River Cotton Mills, Harry Bailey, and myself;'the observers
were Frank Winterbottom of England, Bharat Ram and M. D. Parekh of India,
and Andrew S. Young and Sih Zung Yang of China, who had considerable experi-
ence in the textile trade and markets of the Far East.
My assignment was the rayon industry. We decided to work in teams,
and as Dr Parekh was a graduate of M. I.T. in chemical engineering .including
rayon, I found him to be a most valuable team-mate to survey that industry.
When we went into the field to see the plants, I would go through with the
management, taking photographs and noting technology, machines, layout, capa-
city, materials, research, labor, costs, and such general information, while
Dr Parekh went through with the chief chemist and noted procedures, formulae,
time, temperature, batches, and so on. We would then combine our notes after-
ward into a report on each plant.
We had more than a month's notice before the Misaion left the United
States and I used the time to gather all possible information on the rayon .in
dustry of Japan Colonel Stephen Kennedy of the Office of the Quartermaster
General. who did such excellent work in organizing the textile missions to
Germany compiled a questionnaire on the textile industry of Japan, which
proved to be most helpful to this mission. We undertook to answer all the
questions in it, and so far as rayon is concerned I believe the answers are
fairly complete
There were numerous directories and reports on the rayon industry
of Japan in and out of our government offices and I gathered all that I
knew about, including those of Stanley Hunt of Textile Economics Bureau.
2 -

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.99)
Studies of PEA and the Tariff Commission on rayon
as well as on the trade of Japan were made available to w
them with us. In addition, I had the opportunity to see somo
had been in Japan and who were most helpful with information and
gestions.
The Mission left Washington by Air Transport Command, Tuesday,
January 15th The trip to Tokyo took just 50 hours in the air. The
elapsed time was five days, after losing a day at Topeka for weather and
another at Hamilton Field San Francisco We flew in C 54 transports,
Douglas four motor planes of forty passenger capacity.
After leaving Hamilton Field we flew day and night, stopping
only long enough to refuel The route was via Hickam Field, Hawaii,
Johnston Island Kwajalein, Guam, and Atsugi Field, Japan, making a
total of four stops between the United States and Japan. The shorter
route via Wake and Marcus had just been discontinued when we went out.
The trip was interesting and pleasant. We had heard much about
bucket seats and dreaded being assigned to such a plane, but after fly-
ing across the continent in plush seats we had their nearest equivalent
to bucket seats, made of pipe and canvas, on the hop to Hawaii. Within
a few minutes after reaching altitude, blankets were spread on the deck
and five card games were underway We found them to be more comfortable
and more sociable than the plush jobs in several respects, particularly
as to sleeping on the deck, which is difficult in a seat.
We flew over the Rockies at night, by full moon, with snow on
the land, and it was an inspiring experience. From Hamilton Field we
flew into the setting sun with beautiful cloud formations. In the moon-
light on Oahu the surf and shore. the shoreline drive, the mountains and
the city of Honolulu were all plainly visible as we crossed to a landing
3

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
at Hickam Pield after midnight. There we waited in the pelm
the full moon directly overhead until the take-off a couple of hours
later, At Johnston Island we had breakfast under a sky of all colors
surrounded by a sea that was unbelievably blue.
Over the Marshalls we saw a number of atolls, each e gom of many
colors surrounded with white surf and set in blue ocean, We were remind-
ed of the fighting that recently had taken place on the atolls by the fact
that we could not fly over one where the Japanese were still firing on
planes Kwajalein 1s only one of the many atolls, and the landing strip
is almost all its width from sea to lagoon, but the difference is that
the lagoon, about half the size of Long Island Sound, is the largest in
the Pacific and it provides a busy harbour at Kwajalein
We reached Guam after midnight and also after having skipped
a day on the calendar There it was warm and tropical. and the Southern
Cross dominated the sky
After dawn. on the way to Japan we passed over Iwo Jima and a
number of volcanic islands including one called "Lot s Wife," which is
well named for it resembles a pillar of gleaming salt standing on tho
ocean
On the thirty mile ride by bus from Atsugi Field to Tokyo through
Yokohama we had our 1irst, and the most vivid, impression of what the air...
raids had done to Japanese cities Yokohama and Tokyo were practically
continuous in .industrial plants, docks, and city, but we found them a con...
tinuous stretch of destruction We had seen air views of the results of
the raids, and many photographs from the ground, but even from the air the
camera is limited in scope, and a drive of an hour straight through thirty
miles of rubble gives an impression that does not register in any other way.
4

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.98)
Many concrete buildings remained, and in Tokyo a good part of the centrel
business section escaped but very little that was industrial or inflem
mable remained
Upon traveling more than twenty two hundred miles in Japan and
seeing many citles and towns it was evident that Japan's industries were
badly damaged The cultural citles of Kyoto and Nara, and the shrine tom
of Nikko without war industries were not destroyed but the others visit
ed even to sma? cuuntry towns where here had been war plants, and ainglo
plants in the country side had been bumbed
Upon arriva? in Tokyo we reported tc SCAP and made the acquaint
ance of Major Genera: Wm F Marquat Chief of Eccnomic and Scientific
Section which inc udes a!l industry and expcrt import of Major Harold
Tate Chief of he Textile Branch of that Section, and of Richard May,
Chief of he Exp rt Import Division We spent a day in meeting the or
ganizat: on and in get ing settled in an office in the Dai Ichi Building,
GHQf SCAP
On he  nd day the Mission met with the officials of the Japan
Textile Assoc:a'on the rganization which has combined all previous tex
tile cuntrol assoc:atrons and with a Japanese government representative of
the Minis ry of Commereo and Industry That association is divided into
departments for the various branches of the tex!ile industry
I made he acquaintance of Mr Ohata Head of General Affairs and
of the Rayon and Pu p Department He was formerly of the Teikoku Jinken
Co L'd the ldeut and ?argest rayon company in Japan He was in charge
of much of the conr of the industry and of the scrapping programwhich
desiroyed the majr par f he ndustry during the war He speaks English
quite we! and we spent much time together during my entire stay in Japan
He provided the 'atist cs and inf mation I required of the Association,
5

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.97)
and arranged meetings and trips for me to get other information from the
companies and the plants
The Mission planned its work in three phases, first, a broad sur-
vey of the textile industry and of general related information available in
Tokyo at the Associat lon the Japanese government and in SCAP; second 
period in Osaka the center of the textile industry, to see the company of
ficials and the plan:s. and final!y a return visit in Tokyo to summarize
our findings and write he repor
In the first phase. undertouk t find out the actual production
capacity of the rayon industry in Japan We had been puzzled here by the
tremendous reported capacity which production figures never remotely ap
proached In 939 the industry had a registered capacity of over one bil-
l:on six hundred m::on puunds not only considerably more than any other
country but m re +han a': the :hers combined In i938 Japan 8 peak year
of rayon produc':r. 585 mi!lion pounds was only sl:ghtly over a third of
+he capaci*y reg: 'ered ·he f'lowing year I! was suspected that in some

6

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
the scrapping and the obsolete category accounted for the major portion of
the l939 billion and six hundred million registered capacity. War dameg
accounted for only a nominal percentage, far less than the proportion in
the cotton industry A reserve of machinery was reported, which capacity
could be placed in position to operate if given some parts and equipment.
We found that the actual production of yarn averages only a cer.
tain percentage of registered capacity, and staple evena smaller percentag
We were able then to reduce the registered reserve capacity to actual pro
duction quantities and by adding it to the actual capacity reported, to a
rive at a figure which represents Japan s potential annual rayon productive
capacity as of January
That figure as reported by SCAP in Tokyo, was 354 million pounds,
and it was the essential figure reported as a guide in administering the
rayon industry in Japan Of that potential capacity 12o million pounds is
in yarn machinery and 234 million in staple We do not know what percent
age that tota? is of Japan s past maximum capacity but we do know that
1t is about 60% f her maximum production
One other statistical point is of interest The maximum produc
tion of 585 mil?1on pounds in 1938 was more than enough to supply the
needs of Japan and her export markets at that time. for in that same year,
while production capacity was still increasing a large part of yarn pro
duction machinery was sea'ed and the warehouses were filled with the larg
est stocks of rayon fibe: and fabric in the.history of her industry A
very good reason for +he large stocks was the shrinking market just when
the production of rayon was increasing The market decline was not due
to any less demand for rayon but to the fact that Japan s invasion of
China closed scme of +he markets to her goods
7

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
The lesson to be learned from that situation in Japem's rayom
industry during the years preceding World War II, is that there is a limit
beyond which production of rayon in Japan becomes a burden to ber own eee-
omy, as well as to that of the rest of the world. Regardless of how the
control is established, whether by good business judgement of the Japanese
industry. management by their association regulations by Japanese Govermment,
or by the Supreme Command of the Allies in the Pacific, there should be som
control to prevent another over building of the industry. If there should
be a determination of the level at which Japan 8 rayon, or any other in
dustry should operate it must take into account the current economic
needs of Japan and her potential foreign markets Our Mission did not make
specific recommendations but gave the essential information upon which
such decisions can be made
On February 2nd the Misslon moved to Osaka the center of the tex
t.1le industry There we !ived at the New Osaka Hotel. the newest in Japan,
and had offices in the Sumitomo Building. one of the Zaibatsu companies
which had copper m.nes and various war industries
Soon after our arrival the rayon producers gave us a luncheon, and
I spent the whole day with them discussing the industry Any doubts about
language were overcome at that meeting They had appointed as spokesman,
Mr Tashiro President of Toyo Rayon Co who had represented Mitsui in New
York. and who speaks excellent English After he had presented a very able
summary f the current position of the industry and the discussion had be
come general other producers entered into the conversations. and from then
on I found no difficulty in conversing with any of the companies When I
made a statement as to the purpose and plan of the Textile Mission in Japan,
and they learned that we were there to help them start the industry to op
erate again and not to close it completely. it was natural to find them
8

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.98)
responsive and cooperative. The completely cooperative smd friemily
attitude encountered by the Mission everywhere.in Japan was constemt
evidence of the successful occupation program which General MacArther
has been conducting in Japan.
It is also interesting that the Japanese are taught English as a
required course in public school. While only a minority have had an op-
portunity to use it, most can make themselves understood in basic sub-
jects. In driving out to Tsuda College, some thirty miles in the coun-
try from Tokyo, we asked the way eight times and all but one elderly
woman, who shook her head, replied with understandable directions. Fur-
thermore, the directions of all seven agreed, which is better than the
average in this country.
At the industry meeting we gave the producers a list of the
subjects in which we were interested. We told them that they,could give
us the information in any form most convenient to them, and that we would
fill in any gaps on visits to plants or meetings later. We found in Japan
a possible danger of the Japanese being questioned to death. There was
an occupation survey of war industry, a survey of all industry, a bomb-
ing survey, a reparations survey; there were surveys for science, educa-
tion, research, and labor; there were surveys for missions, commisaions,
the Army, the Military Government, and the economists. In Tokyo we found
much information that was helpful to our Mission, but some, which had
been gathered and might have been useful, had been forwarded to Washing-
ton and was not available in Tokyo.
After interviewing staffs of the various companies in Osak, we
undertook to visit their rayon plants. In addition to many ailk, cotton,
woolen, finishing, and o:her textile plants, we visited one synthetic fiber
plant for polyvinyl alcohol fiber, a calcium carbide plant of interest in
 9 -

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.99)
the production of synthetic fiber, and twenty rayon plants, imoluding ail
of the operable rayon plants but four, and eight which had been converted
to otber industries.
As we knew to be the case before the war, we found no acetate
rayon production. There had been a small semi works production on Kyushu
which had closed when the cotton linters supply ceased The one cupra
rayon plant is still operating, although its capacity suffered under the
scrapping program in proportion All the rest is viscose, with a larger
staple capacity tnan yarn
The present production is only nominal because of coal and salt
shortages Pulp figures published up to the war show that Japan could
produce enough pulp to supply the quantity of rayon capacity which now
remains, and reports on the present pulp industry in Japan verify that
the capacity is still there
The present quality of rayon is below that which could be pro
duced, and which can be produced again, given adequate raw materials. The
quality before the war was various Some mills were making excellont yarn
and staples, and there were various levels of quality from the best to the
worst No rayon is being bleached at present, as chlorine is not available.
No high tenacity is being produced, as coal is too scarce for the hot bath
required, and no cotton linters have been available for cupra production or
high tenacity yarns aince the war started
Price was formerly based on quality, and the quality of all staple
production was determined at the Experimental Station of the Textile Industry,
at Yokohama A committee of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry of the
government determined the price at one of five levels for each product of
rayon staple offered for sale. They had a detailed standard for grading
Not only did grades below standard quality receive a lower level of price,
10

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
but grades above the standard were awarded premium prices.
Tests for staple strength and elongation are made on MoKensie
single fiber testers. Yarn tests are made .in the single on a Japanese
instrument similar to Suder, and in the skein on one similar to Soott
and to Goodbrand, arc type, constant rate, varying load. They have no
incline testers, although the single fiber tester with a rooker arm .is
similar in results. The standard rayon test is at 65% relative humidity,
20° C. The room is air conditioned, double windowed, and on a separato
air system from the rest of the building. Instrument tables are of solid
concrete 4 3/4 inches thick, on pillars resting upon rock foundation. The
floors are cork covered.
This Yokohama Station also conducts research in textiles for the
benefit of the entire industry. It has four departments, with complete
textile equipment for experimentation. First is research on phyaical prop-
erties of fibers, yarns, and fabrics which uses the testing laboratory al-
ready mentloned. Second is research in pulp and in artifical fibers, vis-
cose and acetate yarn and staple. The third is research in spinning on
the silk system only, tow breaking on the Perlok type of system, winding,
weaving: and hosiery knitting. The fourth is scouring, dyeing, finishing,
andbleaching.
In this Station we saw the negative results of attempts to make
textile fiber from mulberry trees. Those trees were largely cut during
the war to force the farming population to seek employment in war industries.
They were dyeing cotton with acid dyes after impregnating thiourea.
In printing they were getting complete penetration of the fibers and fabric
by the use of wetting agents They were printing scarves and handkerchiefs
by rotary block printing an Alsace-Lorraine machine, and copper roll print-
ing on a machine by Alsacienne de Construction Mechaniques of Mulhouse. They
-11

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.99)
were screen printing through silk bolting cloth and through wax paper
stencils on bolting cloth This screen printing included designs in
fabric effects by making the screen from the photograph of such intri-
cate textiles as lace
A new plisse method was being perfected using calaium nitrate
or calcium chloride on silk
Calcium thiocyanide had been found to be
too toxic and the nitrate is most favored Japanese patent No 1ll,700
covers the process
They were mak:ng a combination silk and rayon in which the
former was creped and the latter not By the use of a stencil, with such
a pattern as polka dots the silk was shrunk by chemicals which did not
shrink the rayon
At the Kyoto Imperial Univeraity we visited the Institute of
Fiber Chemistry, a part of the Engineering Department, and a sister of
the Institute for Chemical Research which is also a part of the univer
sity organization We spent an afternoon with Professor Kita, the found
er, now retired. and Professor Horio, the present Director of Research of
Kurashiki Kenshoku Co one of the most progressive of the rayon companies
At this Institute much research on rayon has been done and is in
progress, and polyvinyl alcohol fiber also is a subject of research. Low
caustic is the largest and most active project on rayon at present. Caustio
is so scarce that the project was born of necessity The work involves the
entire industry and industry wide meetings have been held within the past
year to discuss and compare results
Low acid and low temperature bath is a research project found ac-..
tive in the industry as a result of the low caustic
Most of the industry was found to be making xanthate and viscose
12

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.99)
.in a closed single vessel, a system found in Germany by the missions these
One plant was found to be making rayon by the short age method, without.
stopping the flow of alkali cellulose for aging. but keeping it .in motiom
practically all the way into viscose.
Cake washing was found in one rayon plant, but most are still pro-
cessing skeins.
Continuous steeping was found in one plant, but they preferred the
steep press method, posaibly because of the quality of the pulp available.
The continuous steeping was with powdored pulp, which was said to be of a
grade not entirely suited to the process.
Many rayon staple producers finish the fiber before cutting. Som
produce it both ways, depending upon the type of staple required. Excollent
crimped viscose staple is being produced, and high tenacity staple has been
produced also The finishing of the fiber bundle continuously is the process
with the greatest variety of machines found in Japan. There were hardly any
two alike. Only where the fiber bundle was to go through the Perlok type of
process was it dried in that form. One such dryer was seen, while all the rest
were staple dryers
Many rayon mills in Japan are integrated, and some are integrated
backwards as well as forwards. Plants were found which included on the same
grounds sulphuric acid, caustic soda, and carbon disulphide production. Two
rayon plants, by the way, included carbon disulphide recovery units which were
extremely simple Some plants included textile mills for spinning and weav.
ing One plant had a soybean protein extraction mill, and they produced a
30% protein viscose rayon staple The plant of Toyo Rayon at Matsuyama, on
Shikoku, is the only one of its kind in the world It goes from wood pulp to.
finished garments The garments at the present time are allocated by the Home
Department of the Japanese Government through the Governor of the Prefecture
13

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.98)
to the many homoless people.
In quantity it seems that Japan will not be able to floed the mr
kets of the world again at least for many years. In quality their rayon and
rayon pulp can be improved in some respects, and the improvements probably
will be made when materials are again available and they can operate. In price,
they will probably continue below the rest of the rayon producing countries,
even though all costs have risen since the war. In technology they have made
a number of improvements, although the machines were all imported originally
and copied In research they are active and eager for contacts with the rest
of the world from which they have been isolated for nearly five years. In
finance they are threatened by the huge cost of maintaining organizations
and plants with only nominal production, or none at all.
We can only touch upon some of the outstanding subjects here. Fuller
details on the rayon industry of Japan will be released later. Meanwhile, in
closing, I wish to mention a strong impresaion I gained while on this mission
in Japan. Everywhere we went, Army and Military Government officers and men
were found doing a conscientious job, in many cases working twelve hours a
day every day in the week. The occupation is a thankless job for the indi.
vidual. for it is almost impossible to see results as soon as one would de-
sire. The occupation is a tremendous undertaking, with little opportunity
for full recognition of all the individual effort that goes into it. But so
far, in spite of great difficulties, it is successful, and we at home can
work to keep it so. It is as important to support the occupation, which will
be necessary for some time to come, as it was to support the war effort of
our forces fighting abroad Not only do they need young men abroad to fill
the ranks of the occupying forces, but they need men from various lines of
industry who know the business involved. Just as men with special experience
14

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.93)
and akility were loaned to our government during the war, so mheal
oooupeste
panies be willing, if called upon, to loan them now for the
abroed.
We found everywhere in Japan an open and inquiring mind about
our country and the principles on which it operates. We were asked pertieu-
larly about democracy and what it means. It made us aware of the fast that
the United States is an example, which is being studied by others, and it
made us aware also of what a tremendous responsibility we have in that
position.

COMBINED TEXTILE COMMITTEE

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Thorp:
894.655/6
In a meeting of the Combined Textile Committee yesterday, at
which the representatives of the State Department were present, the ques-
tion was raised as to the progress made in completing arrangements for
the shipment of cotton textiles from Japan. As you recall, the Committee
submitted a recommendation to the State Department proposing a distribu-
tion by countries of l20 million yards of cotton textiles which were reported
as available for export in the 2nd quarter of this year. This recommendation
was acknowledged in your letter of May 2l. It is our understanding that, as
yet, none of these textiles have been offered to importing countries in the
Far East.
In view of the serious shortage of textiles in the Far East,
particularly in food-producing areas, members of the Committee requested
me to convey to you their grave concern with the situation and asked me
to do everything possible to assist in expediting the arrangements for the
actual disposal and export of these goods from Japan. The Committee further
recommends that stocks ear-marked for export should be frozen in order that
CS/RH
they do not disappear into domestic consumption in Japan. Allocations of
member countries of the Committee (the U.S., U.K., Canada, India and France)
to the Far Eastern countries are dependent upon the supplies to be made
available from Japan. It is, therefore, most important that we be informed
as to the final disposition of the stocks of Japanese cotton textiles in
making current plans for the 3rd quarter allocations of member countries.
I realize the coordination of the Japanese export program is a
complicated one, but I am sure that by getting the responsible parties to-
894.655/
gether a modus vivendi can be worked out in order that cotton textiles ear-
marked for export are shipped in the very near future to those areas which
are now in critical need. As one method of securing more direct action,
the British member of the Committee strongly advocated that a purchasing
mission, composed of representatives of the Far Eastern countries inftsrested
in purchasing textiles, be sent immediately to Japan to inspect the goods
available for shipment and conclude purchase arrangements with the Us.C.
/6-446
The French and Indian members concurred in this view. It was agreed how-
ever ithatithi motion would be held in abeyance until-th
tuationoould

Dear Mr. Whitman:
Confirming my talk with you this morning, I believe the Institute
is ready to publish the report of the Textile Mission to Japan,
provided the Government is not planning a large printing of same.
We would handle this in much the same way as we are handling the
.655/6-104
report of the Synthetic Fiber Developments in Germany, and the
report by Mr. Wickliffe Rose on the Synthetic Industry in Japan.
We charge a minimum to cover our expense of publishing and dis-
tribution, and are doing this as a service to the industry.
It would appear that the report of the Textile Mission is of
sufficient importance that it should be given a fairly wide
distribution. I would like actually to see the report before
giving a definite acceptance. I hope you have been able to
get copies to me in the mail this afternoon.
Sincerely yours,

I have the honor to refer to my despatch no. 2813 of May 30, 0p
1946, concerning the Japanese textile production position, and to
enclose five copies each of two news items from the Timem of Indis
of June 20 and 21, 1946, respectively, on that subject.
After recapitulating quantities and types of cloth to be
m
shipped from the United States to Japan, the figures for which
should be available to the Department, the first article states
that shortage of spare parts and fittings is retarding developmest
DEPARTMENT OF STAT
NUG 1 6 1946
of the small and middle-sised weaving firms. Necessary steps are
REPORTING SERVICES
Divistón OF FOREIGN
JUL 1 3 1946
being taken to ease the shortage.
The same article quotes the Nippon Senvi Shimbun, Japanese
Textile Association publication, to the effect that the chemical
fibre trade is urging preparation for the revival of commeree to
come from an export standpoint. Three major problems now face the
rayon industry, that is "unification of standards, establishmemt of
a production control system, and reconstruction of the export organi-
sation". Japan's future in the rayon industry, the correspondent
states, depends on raising both qualitative and quantitative standards
to achieve this end. The Senvi Shimbun recommends centralisation of
production management, preferably under the supervision of the Tertile
Association.
The item of June 21 reports results of an experiment undertaken
by a Tokio mill to operate on a four-shift basis. The article des-
cribes the schedule of four six-hour work periods as successful. The
item continues by outlining measures being undertaken to increase
taxtile

are now in existence and about 42 percent of pre-war meshimery
is now available for use". Machinery scrapping "aceowsted for

I have the honor to refer to my despatches nos. 2877 and 2884
of June 21, and 24, 1946 respectively, concerning Japanese indus-
try and to enclose a copy of a news item from the Times of Indis
of July 2, 1946, entitled "Production of Rayon Varn".
ye600.049
The article reports that the production of rayon yarn in
667/26
Japanese mills amounted to a little more than half a million pounds
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
in April. This output was double the production for February but
REPORTING SERVICES
DIVISION OF FOREIGN
JUL 251946
is still a small fraction of operating capacity.
Due to the absence of wood-pulp supplies there had been no
production of rayon pulp for some months and mills have had to use
their own stocks sparingly. According to the article a small
amount of pulp was produced in May and contimuation of the trend
CS/V
in rayon production after the April-June quarter will depend on con-
tinuation of availability of rayon pulp supplies.
The article further states that caustic soda stocks have
dropped to one normal month's supply. The original 3,600 tons'
allotment previously announced was reduced to 2,000 tons for the
quarter. Such a reduction renders the production situation precarious.
It should be noted that the article gives rayon industry statistics
for the month of April.
As stated in my despatches under reference it would be appre-
ciated if the Department would inform me as to whether the Consulate
General should contimue sending these items on Japanese production.

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to despatch no. 2902 dated July 2, 1946, frem Boward
Donovan, American Consul General, Bombay, India, entitled
"Production of Rayon Yarn in.Japan".
Copy:mc
TIMFS OF INDIA - July 2, 1946
PRODUCTION OF RAYON YARN
Japanese Output Doubled
TOKIO: Rayon yarn production by Japanese mills doubled from
February to April, as mills received some shipments of coal allotted
by the Government for the current quarter, according to. figures
published by the Japan Textile Association. The output of a little
more than half a million pounds for the month of April was still
only a small fraction of operating capacity.
There had been no production of rayon pulp for several months,
due to the absence of supplies of pulp wood. Mills have had to
draw sparingly on their own stocks. It was reported that  small
amount of pulp was produced in May. Trade sources said that con-
tinmuation of the upward trend in rayon production after the April-
June quarter would depend on contimuation of this production.
Caustic Soda Stocks
Mill stocks of caustic soda dropped to a month's normal
supply level, and the trade considered this situation precarious.
They were expecting deliveries against the Government'a allotment
of 2,000 tons for the quarter, although there was some 'disappointment
expressed when the quantity allotted was cut from the 3,600 tons
previously announced.
With raw silk frozen hy SCAP directive, weavers turned to rayon
to fill the gap to some extent. Cloth production in April was more
than half a million yards over March's figure.
The following are rayon industry statistics for the month of
April. Cloth figures in thousands of square yards; others in

June 21, 1946 ând to subsequent despatches from the Cánsulate
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
General concerning textile production in Japan, and to enelose
five copies of an article from the Timee of India of July 3,
REPORTING SERVICES
DIVISION OF FOREIGN
AUG 7 - 1946
1946 reporting a decrease in Japanese cloth production.
The article reports that due to the decrease.in mills'
stocks of raw materials, Japanese cotton yarn production
declined during the month of'April. Optimistfc reports in
March concerning the arrival of American cotton proved to b
unfounded. Smaller stocks of mills at the end of March
consisted of two-thirds Egyptian cotton and one-third American
staple. As a consequence the tendency to conserve cotton stocks
suitable for mediym count yarns pending the arrival of American

I have the honor to refer to my despatch no. 2877   2
OF STATE
1946 and to subsequent despatches on the Japanese cotten and sayem
industry, and to enclose five copies of an article from the a
of India of July 25, 1946 entitled "Japan's Exports of Knitted
Ge n/6
Goods".
The article states that knitegoods produotion, which had
largely been built up on the export market, dropped from 60,000,000
dosen pieces in 1937 to around one tenth of that in 1945.
Restrictions were placed on the industry in 1937 at the beginning
of the China incident. Since 1938 there has been a prokibition om
the installation of new knit-goods machinery, except fer military
purposes. During the war, fine-gauge machinery was sorapped due
to the accent placed on heavier military goods.
The article states the Tokyo area, as a result of air raids
during the war, suffered a 50 per cent decrease in knitting 
machinery. Osaka machinery was reduced by 1l per cent and Nagoya's
by 15 per cent. As a result of these attacks, the industry was
dispersed during the war making a revival more difficult. Two other
factors adding to the rehabilitation problem in the knitted goods
industry were mobilisation of labor for war time needs and con-
version of some of the plants to war industries.
The article concludes by giving comparative figures for pro-
duction in 1937 and in 1945.

Dear Mr. Whitman:
Concerning the demand for copies of the report of our
Textile Mission to Japan by various people in the industry, we
have not yet heard whether copies are available.
You will recall that we offered to have the report
published for distribution by the Textile Research Institute,
but we were told that you had decided to publish the 5,000
copies by off-set method and make it available generally.
Please advise what is the status of that project. If it is
side-tracked and you have changed the plan about publishing it,
then the Textile Research Institute would still be interested
in giving it wide distribution. It is unfortunate that there
has been such delay in view of the public interest in the sub-
ject, as well as interest among various departments of the
Government. I am doing all I can to stimulate and maintain pub-
lice interest in a constructive program in Japan and everywhere
I speak on the subject, I am asked for copies of our Mission's
CS/ V
report. The presentation at the Tariff Commission last Wednesday,
July 24th, was the 14th address I have made on the subject, and
there were various government departments present at that meeting.
Some of them are scheduled to go to Tokyo and certainly should
have access to all the material that developed.
Sincerely yours,

DEPAHIMENI UR SIATE
Dear Mr. Secretary:
.655/8-646
You will recall our earlier conversations with and about Mr. Peter F.
Magagna, who subsequently went to Japan to join the staff of General
MacArthur as a silk expert and advisor.
You will recall that I suggested to Mr. Magagna that he feel free to
write me of his experiences during the course of his volunteer work in the
Japanese occupation.
From time to time, I have heard from Mr. Magagna, and after his early
trials and tribulations, which I have mentioned to you from time to time
and to attaches of the State-Department here in Washington, Mr. Magagna,
in association with General Marquat of General MacArthur's staff, proceeded
to devote himself to the textile problems of the occupation government,
with special reference to silk. To that end, I am enclosing a copy of a
plan or program prepared by Mr. Magagna, dealing with the silk and textile
economy of the Japanese under the present circumstances.
CS/A
Needless to say, Mr. Magagna's communications to me by letter and cable
and several telephone calls from Tokyo since his arrival in Japan the latter
part of February of this year, indicated clearly that he was experiencing
many of the difficulties I felt sure would be encountered. Some of these
were quite natural and to be expected under the difficult circumstances to
be experienced in the military occupation of an Army country; other of his
difficulties apparently were the result of the actions and reactions of
various individuals of the Army and Civilian personnel associated with the
Japanese Occupation Government. Apparently an immediate and very important
b8
matter calling for re-examination is that of the relationship between the
United States Commercial Company, the War Department and the State Department.
Unquestionably, necessity being the mother of invention, the United States
6
Commercial Company was properly created to fill a very important need and
5
to perform a vital service. Especially was this so with reference to the
 Unit 
5
economy of enemy-occupied territories.
ED
LDt6
8
L0.14
V.TVA

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.99)
Honorable Dean Acheson
2
August 6, 1946
This is not intended in any way as a criticim of the fine work done by
United States Commercial Company under the presidency of the distinguished
Mr. DeWitt C. Schieck. The problem, rather, is whether or not under all of
the circumstances, United States Commercial Company, at this time, has served
its purpose with reference to the Japanese occupation - more particularly with
reference to that part of the Japanese economy which concerns the silk industry
both in Japan and in the United States.
In view of the recognized abilities of Mr. Magagna as an expert on problems
dealing with the silk industry, and in view of the additional fact that he was
accredited by the Army to General MacArthur's staff for the purpose of advising
on silk, I feel that I should transmit to you, at this time, copies of letters
recently received by me from Mr. Magagna dealing with this problem. I am
attaching, as well, copies of communications bearing directly upon this particular
subject which you may have already received through channels; but which I am at-
taching nevertheless as a matter of convenience.
I am sending copies of this letter to you and all of the other matters at-
tached hereto to General Hilldring, in view of the fact that he was Chief of
the Army Civil Affairs Section at the time Magagna was assigned to General
MacArthur's staff; to John Carter Vincent of the State Department, since he
had some conversations with Magagna before he left for Japan; to Mhin Martin
of Mr. Clayton's Economic Section, since he, as well, participated in these
earlier conversations with Magagna; to the Secretary of War; and to General
Echols, since he is now Chief of the Civil Affairs Section, and with whom I
had some conversations with reference to Magagna's duties; and to Mr. DelWitt
Schieck, who is aware of the entire situation as the result of a recent conver
sation I had with him and one of his associates in his Washington office.
I felt that a careful reading of Magagna's letters and the supporting
attached documents, plus information I have obtained with reference to the silk
industry's market and production in the United States, especially from Mr.
Paolino Gerli, would indicate very strongly that a conference of the officials
directly concerned of the War Department, State Department and United States
Commercial Company should be held without further delay to consider whether or
not Magagna's opinions and conclusions have sufficient merit to warrant eliminat-
ing of the United States Commercial Company in the Japanese silk trade at this
time and the substitution of some other method, either that suggested by Magagna
or some other in order that the declared objects of the Japanese occupation can
be best served. Needless to say, I am greatly impressed by the case made out
by Mr. Magagna. I feel that a conference of the State Department, War Depart-
ment and United States Commercial Company should call in representatives of the
silk industry of the United States and get their reactions on the problem of
testing, shipping and disposal, either by sealed bids, open auctions, et cetere,
and, as well, obtain the views of the industry on silk generally.
I understand, of course, that neither the Army or the State Department, or
both together, have any desire to become merchandisers. That is commendable.

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.99)
Honorable Dean Acheson
-3-
August 6, 1946
Hence the United States Commercial Company. But, by the same token, the
United States Commercial Company may no longer be the proper agency, and it
is entirely possible that neither the State Department nor the War Department,
under the circumstances, can be the best judge, at this time, of the textile
economy, especially in silk, of the present and future Japanese-United States
economic relations. It is, of course, impossible to separate this element from
the political problems involved.
I am aware of your intense interest in this situation and, as you know,
I have implicit confidence in your understanding of this one phase of this
extremely involved Far Eastern picture.
I would like to reiterate that, in my judgment, Mr. Magagna is intensely
sincere in his desire to serve his Country in the field in which he is best
qualified - as a silk expert and advisor to SCAP. I feel any statements or
reports he makes will be made honestly to further the work being done by
General MacArthur in the difficult task of the Japanese occupation. I am sure
the various Government departments concerned will welcome any information that
will aid them in executing the policy of our Government with reference to the
Japanese economy and if and when the facts warrant it, to revise that policy
accordingly.
If, in the opinion of the silk industry and the advice of the experts and
textile people with General MacArthur's staff, the United States Commercial
Company has served the purpose for which it was created, as far as this item
of silk, at least, is concerned, and possibly other items, than I believe
Mir. Schieck of the United States Commercial Company, for whom I have a very
high regard and who has been very courteous and cooperative with me, would be
the first to agree to the cessation of activities of that organization. If
the contrary is concluded, that is all very well; but it seems the situation
is such, at the moment, that the problem should be most carefully re-axmined
and determined.
But, under no circumstances, should the opinion of Mr. Magagna be ignored
because of the clashing of personalities which may have resulted during as-
sociations in the past several months in the occupation government.
Magagna is able and experienced in the silk field, and what is most im-
portant, has been on the ground in Japan since February; he has lived in Japan
for years heretofore and is reasonably conversant with that nation's economy.
Keeping in mind our conversations to the same point of view - it is the long
range program with reference to the Japanese economy and its relationship to
our own which must be paramount and take precedence from time to time as these
difficulties arise.
The silk industry in the United States has the right to a renaissance and
the people in the industry, both management and labor and the communities
chiefly affected, to profit reasonably when it is consistent with the high

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.97)
Honorable Dean Acheson
-4
August 6,
policy of our Govermment. At the same time, the relaticuship of the silk
industry in Japan to the entire Japanese economy is historically and tradi-
tionally of major importance. It is, therefore, necessary and immediate the
this problem be considered acute in contributing to the rebirth of the Japane
nation as a country that can live among the free democratic nations of the
world.
Sincerely yours,
Raniel J. Slood
Daniel J. Flood, M.C.

a matter of time until Kobe would have had to be closed again.
5. Formerly, every exporter made and did business on his own tests. These

whole trade, in Japan and abroad; they created untold confasion in the
qualities. In addition, there was 10 to 12 testing houses in the U.S.
with no better results, causing still worse confusion. In January, 1931,
the Japanese Government decreed that henceforth no business could be
transacted in Japan for export purposes, unless on the besis of the
official tests, to the great satisfaction of domestic producers and
consumers abroad. This forced also the testing houses in the USA to
go out of business one by one, until only the United States Testing
Company remained, ekcing a dwindling existence because American manu-
facturers found it less reliable than the official Japanese tests.
Even the New York SIIK KXCHANGE admitted transactions and deliveries
on the basis of official Japan tests, until the outbreak of the War.
All this proves the reliability of the Japanese official tests .

SUBJECT: Export Program for Silk Fabries and Rayon Fabries
TO : Chief, Econamic and Seientific Section, GHQ, SCAP
1. An export program has been proposed and accepted, presappesing the
sale of greet quantities of fabries to world markets, particularly findsked
silk goods. Nevertheless, in malding up the program nobody has had the fere-
sight to study tariffs that would be placed on thess silk goods by the varieus
countries considered as potential customers. This has been overlooked, im
spite of the fact that the Import-Export Division is supposed to knew all abest
the ramifications of exporting.

war machine. She threw all thought of values to the four winds. She hed silk
textiles of all kinds to sell, and with silk sè ling at a price average ef twe
worth about twenty-five (25) cents at that time we arrive at a figure of

States Treasury Department when I served on various textile tariff committess.

of paying for them, unless they borrow themoney from the United States to do so.
However, exportations of that kind do not help the Japanese or SCAP.
5. Already great yardages of silk piece goods have been seised and are
being held throughout the country. Additional silk yardage is being manufactared,
all for export, and nothing has been done about the vital question of tariffs.
I urge that further production of silk piece goods or combination silk fabries
for domestic or export use be drastically curtailed and that all efforts be made
to send that raw wilk to the United States where it will quickly build up dollar
exchange and relieve the tremendous shortages of fiber which is curtailing pro-
duction and causing unemployment, and because of the lack of consumer goods
is creating dangerous inflation in the United States.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
13 June 1946
SUBJECT: Payment for Export Commodities in Kind.
TO:
Chief, Economic and Scientific Soction, GHQ, SCAP
1. After a series of conferences held with leading Japanese silk
industrialists, who are well known and recognised as authorities on the
subject, both in Japan and the United States, the only conclusion that
can be arrived at is that the raw silk industry of Japan is deteriorating
rapidly because of the lack of a well organised and definite poliey.
Imediate action must be taken to remedy this condition, to revive the
industry and to regain the ground that has been lost.
2. Cocoon farmers and the producers of xaw silk have not been placed
in a position to receive in kind remuneration equal at least to their
expenditures and efforts. These silk peope sitlse that they are en-
countering greet difficulty in purchasing silk pipce goods and rew silk
greatest single source of dollar excharge is being destroyed and the
 sompinuing basis is also being destreyed.
largest non-war potential on
3. If this trend continkas thate will be nothing to offeet the cost
of occupation. It is doubtful thas the American people will tksh to spend
the money necessary to pontinxe the occupation. The fact that the United
States could not contibpe a syccessful occupation after the magnificent
to the prestige of the American people at this time when more than ever
the United States is the keystone and hope of the world for the maintenance
of future peace.
4. Gae way to recover lost ground quickly and the one way that I have
continually advocated is that interim payments be made to the Japanese
producers both in the essential commodities they need and in yen. This
will give a greet incentive to the Japanese people whom we have promised
assistance and liberation under the declaration of Potsdam.
5. Since the start of the silk industry in Japan it has been the
custom of the reeling mills to erect dormitories and bring to the fasteries
the required operatives. Part of their inerement has always been in the
form of food and ledging. This system should be expended to permit payment
in kind in other fields of the silk industry.
6. Let us revert to this principle and utilise the canned food of all
types that is stored here and is now deteriorating, and meskanisal equipment
of all types badly needed by the agriculturists and which is stered here
and on adjacent islands. Why not collect them and barter them to the
producers of silk. A system could be set up without too mmch diffieulty

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.97)
in the varions silk-prodecing prefestures se thet eqditakle barter preeedare
could be carried cut for both silk and silk piese goods. The seme pasters
sould be adopted for various other comodities that are estually is the
sountry but are not eoming eut to the markets because of the imequitable
arrangemente new in force for maiing paymeats.
7. All of this food and meahanisal equipment is rapidly deterierating
and it is not worth the effort or expense to re-ship it to the Uaited ststes.
Why destrey these things as Mr. Royall suggested, or why sell theme te the
Philippines or China for virtually little or nothing. We badly meed silk
and siik fiber in the United States. This arrangemest would be an equitable
arrangement and would bring immediate results. The job of the Amerieam peeple
and the American forces is a successful ccupation of Japen for the future
peace of the world. We should not hesitate or delay eetien in amy way if
that action will serve to assist this occupation. It is irrefatable that the
aims of the war and the Potedem declaration are now being defeated. We are
literally and by indirect methed violating the Potedam Agreement by seimare
of commodities of various kinds. The indirect method we use is scAp direetives
to the Japanese Government to make these seisures. These seisures are made
and proper provisions are not taken to see that an equitable return is made
to the original producers.
8. The actions outlined above wil have a salutory effect on the Japanese
9. During ten months of occupatiom in a country that did have and still
has large stocks of non-war compodlties fer export the Import-Export Division
As a result, we find today that litt)s or no dollar exchange has been created.
The only exchange that has been ermated has been by seisure of Japanese liquid
assets both hereand in the United States.
10. Unfortunately, the morst element in this situation is that the methods
unwisely embarked upon hare titerally disorganised the entire industrial and
agricultural life of Japan. These methods will make the accumulation of
exportable commodities more difficult, since the sources of supply have become
alarmed at the inequitable methods set up for payment. It is becoming more
difficult each day to secure these necessary commodities for dollar exchange.
1l. Anyone making a careful study of this situation must come to the
same conclusion. Therefore, it is necessary that immediate steps be taken to
right this condition.
P. F. Magagna

The Reeling Committee of the Board
of the Raw Silk Industry has formally
raised the question of attempting to de-

accept 1416 denier silk instead of 116
for the remainder of this year. In view

supply. the reeling of 1416 denier is
preferable, the Committee stated. They
pointed out that three to four years
would be required to bring the standards
of silkworm breeding and reeling skills
up to peak pre-war levels.
Plans are being made for the enlarge-

hama Raw Silk Conditioning House, in
order to bring the aggregate testing
capacity of the Yokahama and Kobe
Houses up to 18,000 bales a month. The
testing rate will have to average is,000

rejections, during the second half of

reach 130,000 bales in 1946.

FROM : JK - Mr. Whitman
SUBJECT:
Consultation with SCAP regarding the textile ration for
Japan.
References: FEC O79
C2-020/1
Memorandum to General McCoy dated
September 6, 1946.
Central
RM/R 894.655/9-1146
This Document Must Be Returned to
of Japanese Economy" which included a provision for a
maximum textile ration (FEC O79). The US counter pro-
posal (C2-020/1) 1s much more simplified and amits any
reference to its specific textile ration.
This matter has been discussed in Committee no. 2
for some time and I am convinced that the Committee will
accept the simplified US version of the policy statement
but will insist on the inclusion of some reference to a
maximum textile ration.
It is also my opinion that the Committee might be
persuaded to abandon the idea of putting the maximum
textile ration in the policy statement if an alternativ
method acceptable to the US Government can be found for
communicating their opinions to SCAP. Accordingly I
hereby request authority to propose to Committee no. 2
that their views on the matter be forwarded to SCAP
through the Chairman of the Commission as provided in
paragraph VI (1) of the terms of reference as an
alternative to including the textile ration in the
policy statement.
The exact contents of the message would be a matter
for discussion and agreement. My proposal would be to
make the "consultation" a resume of the opinions and
discussions on the question to include the following
points:
1. A statement of the extreme importance of low
894.655/9-1146
CS/HHH
priced textiles to East Asia and the interest
of the FEC countries in promoting such exports
from Japan.
0017
2. The
An 4---
Cat

=== Page 501 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
2.
The opinion of countries that some kind of
textile ration would be required to insure
that Japan met her obligations under the pol
statement. In this connection, note wouldb
taken of the establishment by SCAp of a 8.9
square yard ration.
3. A statement that a number of governments agreed
with UK's position that standard ration should
probably not be more than 2 1/2 pounds; that
this was the present ration in the UK and that
it was considered to be above a disease and
unrest level.
Any comments on special position which might
be taken by Soviet or other delegation.
5. A statement of the US position to the offoot it
supported the policy; that it considered that
the ration should be set as low as consistent
with meeting the objectives of the occupation
in order to meet other Asia needs and also to
balance import costs; it further considers that
final determination of Japan's minimum needs
is a matter for decision by SCAP

Department of State

A-C
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Dear Mr. Thorp:
894.65
The initial recommendation of the Combined Textile Committee
as to the country distribution of 120 million square yards of Japanese
cotton textile stocks, notified as being available for export in the
second quarter of this year, was sent to you in the Committee's letter
of April 18, 1946. A further communication was sent to you on June 4
commenting on the delay in getting these stocks actually earmarked and movingl
into export. The visible supply up to the end of the third quarter now
appears to be larger and the Committee hopes that the additional supply
will also be expedited to the Liberated Countries of the Far East.
Cables received from SCAP in the last week of July, and
further information included in the tele-conference between the War
Department and SCAP on August 14, indicated that the stocks available
for export were lower than anticipated, but that about llo million yards of
cotton textiles from new production would be available for export in the
S
current quarter. The Committee was, therefore, requested by SCAp to make
a further recommendation.
recommendation covering the first 193 million yards of cotton cloth to be
exported from Japan as well as 8 million pounds of yarn and 800,000 pounds
of singlets.
Will you please have the attached recommendation transmitted
through the appropriate channels for review and subsequent action.
Sincerely yours,
Hechert Toel
