C. Tox. C. Staff Report No. 4, Revised
August 28, 1946
ALLOCATION OF JAPANESE COTTON TEXTTLES - 3rd CUARTER OF 1946
1.
The Staff draws attention to Document No. <3, which restates the avail
ability of Japanese stocks for export and includes a request that the Committee
make recommendations on those immediately available, and Document No. 25, which
outlines the availability from new production for export. It will be seen that
third quarter availability as presently envisaged by the Staff amounts to:
Fabric
- Stocks
83 mm. sq. yds.
- New Production
110
11
Total
193
Yarn
- New Production
a.0 m. lb.
Singlets - New Production
0.8 mm. 1b.
2.
It will be recalled that the Committec in its Rocommendation No. 5
adviscd on the distribution of 120 million yards (including raw material equivalent)
of Japaneso cotton toxtile stocks which wore at that time thought to be available.
In so doing, the Conmittee based its dacision on the world balance sheet and the
urgent need for supplies in the liberated Far East. The Staff reaffirms this
basis and recommonds that tho distribution should apply to the first 120 million
yards of fabric availability whether from stocks or new production.
3.
The Committee has then to consider third quarter availability over and
above the aforementioned yardage. The Staff in weighing this problem, had to bear
in mind the fact that tho second quarter supply from Japan programmed for the
liberated Far East had not become availablo and consequently the serious shortage
in the territories concerned had been aggravated. At the same time, the special
needs of rice producing countries to which the Committee's attention had boen
drawn by the Combined Food Board (Document No. 13) was a factor to be considered.
The Staff, thercfore, recommends that the distribution of the additional 73 million
yards of fabric should gencrally follow the same overall pattern* as that recom-
mended for tho initial 12o million yards, subject to the folloring provisos:
a) that 10 million yards be reserved for distribution to
food producing territories in the Far East by the
Commodity Credit Corporation vhich should notify tho
Committee on the ultimate disposition of this yardage;
b)
that allocations not takon up within a reasonablo time
should be notified by the U.S.C.C. to the Committee
for reallocation. The Committee, in so doing, vould
give special preference to rice producing countries in
the Far East.
4.
The Staff noted that the form of the yarn availability was apparently
well adapted to the necds of the Far East and suggests that in order to further
their welfare and make a contribution to their clothing needs, liberated territories
should participate approximately pro rata to their screened raquirements.
5.
The availability of 800,O00 pounds of singlets (T-shirts) is spocified
in a recent messago from Tokyo. These are being manufactured in August and
September to meet requirements of Burma and Hong Kong. It was felt, however, that
theso singlets if suitable, should also bo mado available to othor Far East terri-
tories having roquiremonts. Accordingly, subjoct to tho deduction of 200,000 pounds
of these singlets which the Commodity Credit Corporation has requested be sot aside
for special progrems in the Far East, the Staff rccommends that this availability
be distributed pro rata to screened requirements of the same area.
* See Staff Mccting Minutes of August 9, 1946.

DEPARIMENT OF STATE
COMBINED TEXTIIE COITTEE
Document No. 25
SUBJECT: Cotton Textiles, Produced in Japan from U. S. Cotton,
Available for Export.
The following is an edited version of a cable sent from SCAP
in Tokyo to the U. S. War Department on the above subject.
Bernard Barton, Secretary
Combined Textile Committee
July 31, 1946
The following gives status of program for manufacturing CCC
cotton. 185,000 bales cotton received by 26 July compared 295,000 ex-
pected per textile mission report. Therefore, manufacturing plan is 45
days behind schedule. Revised estimates of cotton consumption follows
in bales: June, 3,000; July, 30,000; August, 60,000; September, 80,000;
October, 90,000; November, 100,000; December, 110,000,
The above estimates assume an adequate supply of coal and
electric power. Present June-July-August approved manufacturing plan
estimates yarn production equivalent to about l00,o00 bales raw cotton.
The equivalent of 19,000 bales being retained for essential domestic use
and balance of 81,Ooo bales available for export. Breakdown for export
proposed is as follows in percentages: fabric, 89; sale yarn, 10; knit
underwear, 1.. Phe average weekly cotton fabric production in June was
about 2 million linear yards and we expect the following weekly rates:
July, 3 million; August, 10 million; September, 20 million. 3rd quarter
fabrics from all CC cotton estimated at approximately l40 million linear
yards, and at least 1l0 million yards will be available for third quarter
export, one-third in August, balance in September. The types are as
follows in linear yards: print clothes (shirtings), 25 million; medium
sheetings (Class C), 60 million; coarse sheetings (Classes A and B), 14
million; drills, 10 million; miscellaneous, 1 million. Production of the
foregoing types most satisfactory obtainable from cotton arriving first
five cargoes, but estimates are subject to revision when the comprehensive
weaving plan is submitted by the Japanese within 10 days.
We believe the present data are sufficinetly reliable for
Combined Textile Committee allocations by broad classifications to
appropriate countries. The actual sales by U.S. Commercial Company to
be made later when information supplied on quantities on hand by specific
constructions. Proposing American standard constructions as basic and
we will submit list as determined by limitations of Japanese equipment.
If suitable cotton is available, changes can be made in the fourth quarter
schedule if you advise a demand for particular type fabric.
Sale yarn production is available in the third quarter totals
8 million pounds, of which 2 million pounds is now ready for immediate
shipment. Yarn is size 20/1 suitable for knitting or weaving, put up in
skeins, packed in 400 pound bales. Also proposed manufacture about
800,O00 pounds of singlets in August-September for Hong Kong and Burma.
Style and quantity similar to prewar. Samples are being forwarded.
Will forward revised estimates soonest. Request you advise
soonest any changes desired in proposed program. Otherwise, we will
assume that plan proposed satisfactory to you.

FROM : JK - Mr. Whitman
suBJEcT: Request for Entry of Siamese Textile Purchasing Mission into Japan.
The U.S. Legation in Bangkok reports in its Tel 1d59 of September 16
that no authorization has been received from SCAP for entry into Japan of
a Siamese textile purchasing mission. At least two requests for such
authorization have been sent to SCAP through the U. 3. Legation in Bangkok
the second request being sent on August 3 in a telegram repeated to the
Department as No. 904.
894.65
The Combined Textile Committee has given Siam an allocation of
approximately 30,000,000 yards of Japanese textiles out of a total third
quarter allocation of 193,000,000 yards. The Siamese Government does not
have a Mission in the United States qualified to purchase these alloeated
textiles from samples on file in USCC offices here. We know that British,
Hong Kong, and Burmese textile purchasing missions have been in Tokyo as
this fact was reported in the teletype conference of August 14 (wDttk 6815)
9-1946
In view of the abovefacts, the Siamese request for permission to send
a purchasing mission to Tokyo appears to be reasonable. It appears almost
certain that such a mission would greatly expedite the procurement of
Japanese textiles. Accordingly in the following message to SCAP it is
suggested:
DRAFT CABLE
Please advise current status Siamese request to send textile
purchasing mission to Tokyo. Felt here such misdon would result in
principel
earlier shipment textiles to Siam and textiles believed to be ealy
effective inducement to bring forth badly needed Siamese rice.
Repeat Bangkok relegtel 1059 Sept 16.

8

V

BRITISH EMBASSY
Commercial Department,
WASHINGTON 8, D. C.
LNATIONAL RESOURCES DIVISION
No. C.78/254
November 14, 1946.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Dear Mr. Evans,
894.655/1
 I am writing with reference to Mr. Chadwick's letter
of the 2nd November about the prices of Japanese textiles.
I had a talk with Mr. Gardiner about this on Wednesday
the 13th. I was accompanied by Mr. F.R. Turner, who has just
arrived here from London to assist in looking after the Burma
Government and Colonial Office interest in these tertiles.
We raised the question of a reduction in the prices
of the Jipanese stocks on account of the lower quality of those
goods in comparison with comparable United States products. Our
conversation with Mr. Gardiner turned very largely on the contents
of Mr. Chadwick's letter to you. Mr. Gardiner felt that we were
9
reopening the "question of price basis" (I quote from the letter)
in spite of what is there stated.
I think myself that the operative word there is "basis"
We have felt that we must for the present accept the proposed
prices as a basis although they are felt to be high, but mr.
Chadwick's letter went on to say that we "attach great importance
to the necessity for adjustment of prices where the quality is
This consideration arises immediately on the stocks of goods
S
made several years ago from mixed Indian, Burma and American
cotton, and I have to-day received instructions to raise it with
A
you as a matter of real importance.
The proposed prices are perhaps logically defensible
in the case of goods made from United States cotton and comparable
with United States manufacture. Several of the buyer countries
/conside
11946
Mr. James G. Evans,
Department of State,
—9
Washington, D.C.
1446

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
consider them too high, but it can be argued that they are
fair world prices and that S.C.A.P. is entitled to secure
such prices.
If, however, inferior quality goods are priced at
the same level, what explanation is it possible to give?
What can the buyer Governments say, in explanation, to
their traders when offering them goods of differing quality
at identical prices? It will surely be argued that in this
case the U.S.C.C. is being more concerned to secure a world
famine price than a "fair" world price.
Yours very truly,
Pennial
An thong
(A.E.Percival)
Commercial Counsellor.
AEP:LJ
Copy to Mr. A.Z.Cardiner, U.S.C.C.

=== Page 559 === (confidence 0.98)
AMERICAN VISCOSE CORPORATION
P. 0. Box 468
Wilmington 99,Delaware
November 26, 1946
The Textile Mission to Japan, sponsored by the Department of
State and The Army, returned in April of this year, after leaving
with General MacArthur a report of the condition of the textile in-
dustry in that country. That report was forwarded to Washington and,
while it was released to the press through a limited mumber of mimeo-
graphed copies, it has now been printed and is available for fifteen
cents at the office of the
Superintendent of Documents,
U. S. Government Printing Office,
Washington 25, D. C.
The Occupation of Japan is progressing constructively and suc-
cessfully. The outcome is so important to the United States that an
understanding of the various conditions by the people in the United
States can be helpful in our foreign policy.
The condition and the prospects of the textile industry of Japan
"eof relative importance when one realizes that before the war one-
half of the industrial employees were in the textile industry.
tusRot
H. Wickliffe Rose

Dear Paul:
We have discussed the sale of Japanese and German cotton textiles
894.655/12-646
with the governments named on the attached list and in all probability will
be making contracts of sale thereon with such governments or their trade or
supply agencies.
in the absence of execution of such contracts by Ambassadors or
Ministers Plenipotentiary duly accredited to this government by the govern-
ments referred to, which type of execution of the contracts we anticipate
will be the exception rather than the rule, we shall want to be certain that
the representatives of such governments are duly authorized to execute the
contracts on behalf of their governments.
It has occurred to us in the circumstances that it would be appro-
priate for us to requost the Department of State to communicate with such
governments and to request them to supply, for transmittal to us, statements
to the effect that specified persons, duly named, are authorized to bind
their governnents on such contracts and to execute them on their government's
behalf.
e should very much appreciate having the Department of State act
for us in this connection and in a similar respect on possible future re-
quests of the same character.

COMBINED TEXTILE COMMITTEE

JK:whitmanill
Inter-Allied Trade Board for Japan
113147
2516 Massachusetts Avenue, N. W.
Washington, D. C.
894.655
Dear Mr. Whitman:
Since receiving your letter of November 15 the Combined
Textile Committee has given active consideration to the supply
894.655
prospects for the first quarter of 1947.
The export programs of the member countries have been
undergoing substantial revision and it has been impossible, up
to this time, to formulate a recommendation based on the differ-
ential between essential import requirements and the estimated
supplies which may be available, during the coming quarter, to
the countries which should receive Japanese cotton textiles.
2-2046
The Committee has recently been informed that the
original estimate of 70 million yards, assumed to be available
in the fourth quarter over and above the 193 million yards for
which a recommendation was previously made, is now assessed at
less than 50 million yards. Under the circumstances, it is pro-
posed that this amount should be considered as a part of the
available exports for the first quarter of 1947.
The Combined Textile Committee will discontinue its
activities at the end of this year. In view of the situation,
the Committee hopes that future proposals for the distribution
of Japanese-produced cotton textiles available for export in
1947 could be presented to the Inter-Allied Trade Board for
CS/A
concerned as he may consider desirable.
Sincerely,
Herbert Rose, Chairman
894.655
Combined Textile Committee

gh
12.2046

This will acknowledge and thank you for your lettes of
December 17 and December 20 on the subject of distribution of cottem
textiles. While we were originally concerned, when we discussed the
subject with you in November, in obtaining clearance from the Depart-
ment of State for sales to as many countries as possible, rather than
having reviewed once again the recommendations of the Combined Textile
Committee, your statements of needs of various countries has been read
with interest and will, of course, be given all consideration.
We understand from our conversations that you have no objec-
tions to sales to any countries, once those in need have been offered
the goods in question, and your confirmation of this position will be
much appreciated.
Meantime, we understand that the policy of uscc, originally
formulated in concert with the interested agencies of the government,
looking toward forward sales of the textiles to the extent that cotton
JAN16
TLED
prices have been fixed under the CCC contract, still meets with your
concurrence despite references in your letter to the establishment of
"reserves for adjustment."

offevs for the goods is clearly essential, and we are grateful to learn
894.655
from Mr. Whitman that he is proposing to the Trade Board of the Far
Eastern Commission that its members make known no later than Jamuary 13
the quantities of textiles which they are prepared to purchase from the
Japanese production under the terms proposed by us including, of course,
payment in dollars.
